.

S9  Symmetric bandit learning

S9.1
Symmetric bandit learning task instructions

German original
In jedem Durchgang (Trial) dieser Aufgabe können Sie wählen, ob Sie die linke oder die rechte Pfeiltaste drücken. Innerhalb eines Blocks ist jeder Taste eine feste Wahrscheinlichkeit zugeordnet, mit der das Drücken dieser Taste zu einer Belohnung von +1 oder +0 Punkten führt.
Beispiel: In einem Block führt das Drücken der linken Pfeiltaste mit einer Wahrscheinlichkeit von 0,8 zu +1 Punkt und mit 0,2 zu +0 Punkten. Das bedeutet: Im Durchschnitt erhalten Sie bei 10 Durchgängen mit der linken Taste etwa 8-mal +1 Punkt und 2-mal +0 Punkte. Die rechte Taste ist in diesem Fall mit den gegenläufigen Wahrscheinlichkeiten verknüpft (also +1 mit 0,2 und +0 mit 0,8). In jedem Block ist eine der beiden Tasten vorteilhafter, d.h. mit einer höheren Wahrscheinlichkeit für +1 verknüpft – manchmal ist dieser Unterschied offensichtlich, manchmal subtiler. Ihr Ziel ist es, die Taste mit der höheren +1-Wahrscheinlichkeit so oft wie möglich zu drücken. Welche Taste das ist, müssen Sie in jedem Block neu herausfinden, da die Wahrscheinlichkeiten mit Beginn eines neuen Blocks neu vergeben werden. Nachdem Sie sich mit der Aufgabe vertraut gemacht haben, absolvieren Sie 10 Blöcke mit jeweils 30 Trials.

English translation
In each task trial, you can choose between pressing the left or right cursor button. For a given block of the task, each button is associated with fixed
probabilities that pressing it will yield a reward of +1 or +0. For example, on
a given block, pressing the left cursor button will yield a reward of +1 with
a probability of 0.8 and a reward of +0 with a probability of 0.2. When
pressing the left button, you will thus observe a +1 reward on average in 8 out of
10 trials and a reward of +0 in 2 out of 10 trials. Pressing the other button
(in this example, the right button) will yield the rewards with opposite probabilities
assigned to them. In the example, if pressing the left button yields a reward of +1 with
a probability of 0.8, then pressing the right button yields a reward with a probability of 0.2,
and likewise for obtaining rewards of +0. Thus, one of the buttons is always
associated with a higher probability of obtaining a reward of +1 than the
other button. Sometimes this difference is pronounced, sometimes it is more subtle.
Your aim is therefore to press the button with the highest associated probability for +1 as
often as possible. In each block, you need to determine which button it is, as the probabilities
will be reset with the start of a new block. After familiarizing yourself with the task, you will
perform 10 blocks of 30 trials each.

(a)
Symmetric bandit task descriptive statistics and posterior predictive learning functions for participants 1 to 30 (
x
-axis: trial,
y
-axis: observed proportion of maximizing actions).

(a)
Symmetric bandit task descriptive statistics and posterior predictive learning functions for participants 31 to 60 (
x
-axis: trial,
y
-axis: observed proportion of maximizing actions).

Figure S.18:
Symmetric bandit task descriptive statistics for individual participants. On each subpanel, the red line depicts the proportion of maximizing actions across blocks, the dark gray line depicts the average reward per trial obtained by the participant, and the straight light gray line corresponds to the average adjusted probability state across blocks.
The dashed black line depicts the participant-specific posterior predictive learning function, i.e., the probability of the maximizing action based on the participant-specific most plausible model and its maximum likelihood parameter estimates as a function of
t
.

S9.2
Bayesian agent model analysis

Theorem S.6

(Belief state)
.

Let

p(s_{1},d_{1:T},a_{1:T},r_{1:T})\\
=p(s_{1})p(d_{1})p(a_{1}|d_{1})p(r_{1}|s_{1},a_{1})\prod_{t=2}^{T}p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(a_{t}|d_{t})p(r_{t}|s_{1},a_{t})

(S.94)

denote the ABM induced by eq. (
24
), eq. (
40
) and eq. (
41
) and let

p(s_{1}|a_{1:0},r_{1:0}):=p(s_{1})=\mbox{U}(s_{1};[0,1])=\mbox{Beta}(s_{1};\alpha_{1})\mbox{ with }\alpha_{1}:=(1,1)

(S.95)

denote the agent’s block-specific prior probability state distribution. Then for
t=2,...,T
, it holds that

p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})=\mbox{Beta}\left(s_{1};\alpha_{t}\right)\mbox{ with }\alpha_{t}=\left(1+\sum_{k=1}^{t-1}\tilde{r}_{k},t-\sum_{k=1}^{t-1}\tilde{r}_{k}\right).

(S.96)

Proof.

We first note that for
t=2,...,T

p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})=\frac{p(s_{1})\prod_{k=1}^{t-1}p(r_{k}|s_{1},a_{k})}{\int_{s_{1}}p(s_{1})\prod_{k=1}^{t-1}p(r_{k}|s_{1},a_{k})}

(S.97)

because

\displaystyle p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})

\displaystyle=\frac{p(s_{1},a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}{p(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{d_{1:t-1}}p(s_{1},d_{1:t-1},a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}{\int_{s_{1}}\sum_{d_{1:t-1}}p(s_{1},d_{1:t-1},a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{d_{1:t-1}}p(s_{1})p(d_{1})p(a_{1}|d_{1})p(r_{1}|s_{1},a_{1})\prod_{k=2}^{t-1}p(d_{k}|a_{1:k-1},r_{1:k-1})p(a_{k}|d_{k})p(r_{k}|s_{1},a_{k})}{\int_{s_{1}}\sum_{d_{1:t-1}}p(s_{1},d_{1:t-1},a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{d_{1}}p(s_{1})p(d_{1})p(a_{1}|d_{1})p(r_{1}|s_{1},a_{1})\prod_{k=2}^{t-1}\sum_{d_{k}}p(d_{k}|a_{1:k-1},r_{1:k-1})p(a_{k}|d_{k})p(r_{k}|s_{1},a_{k})}{\int_{s_{1}}\sum_{d_{1:t-1}}p(s_{1},d_{1:t-1},a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{d_{1}}p(s_{1})p(a_{1},d_{1})p(r_{1}|s_{1},a_{1})\prod_{k=2}^{t-1}\sum_{d_{k}}p(a_{k},d_{k}|a_{1:k-1},r_{1:k-1})p(r_{k}|s_{1},a_{k})}{\int_{s_{1}}\sum_{d_{1:t-1}}p(s_{1},d_{1:t-1},a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}

\displaystyle=\frac{p(s_{1})p(r_{1}|s_{1},a_{1})\sum_{d_{1}}p(a_{1},d_{1})\prod_{k=2}^{t-1}p(r_{k}|s_{1},a_{k})\sum_{d_{k}}p(a_{k},d_{k}|a_{1:k-1},r_{1:k-1})}{\int_{s_{1}}\sum_{d_{1:t-1}}p(s_{1},d_{1:t-1},a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}

\displaystyle=\frac{p(s_{1})p(r_{1}|s_{1},a_{1})p(a_{1})\prod_{k=2}^{t-1}p(r_{k}|s_{1},a_{k})p(a_{k}|a_{1:k-1},r_{1:k-1})}{\int_{s_{1}}\sum_{d_{1:t-1}}p(s_{1},d_{1:t-1},a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}

\displaystyle=\frac{p(s_{1})p(r_{1}|s_{1},a_{1})p(a_{1})\prod_{k=2}^{t-1}p(r_{k}|s_{1},a_{k})\prod_{k=2}^{t-1}p(a_{k}|a_{1:k-1},r_{1:k-1})}{\int_{s_{1}}p(s_{1})p(r_{1}|s_{1},a_{1})p(a_{1})\prod_{k=2}^{t-1}p(r_{k}|s_{1},a_{k})\prod_{k=2}^{t-1}p(a_{k}|a_{1:k-1},r_{1:k-1})}

\displaystyle=\frac{p(a_{1})\prod_{k=2}^{t-1}p(a_{k}|a_{1:k-1},r_{1:k-1})p(s_{1})p(r_{1}|s_{1},a_{1})\prod_{k=2}^{t-1}p(r_{k}|s_{1},a_{k})}{p(a_{1})\prod_{k=2}^{t-1}p(a_{k}|a_{1:k-1},r_{1:k-1})\int_{s_{1}}p(s_{1})p(r_{1}|s_{1},a_{1})\prod_{k=2}^{t-1}p(r_{k}|s_{1},a_{k})}

\displaystyle=\frac{p(s_{1})p(r_{1}|s_{1},a_{1})\prod_{k=2}^{t-1}p(r_{k}|s_{1},a_{k})}{\int_{s_{1}}p(s_{1})p(r_{1}|s_{1},a_{1})\prod_{k=2}^{t-1}p(r_{k}|s_{1},a_{k})}

\displaystyle=\frac{p(s_{1})\prod_{k=1}^{t-1}p(r_{k}|s_{1},a_{k})}{\int_{s_{1}}p(s_{1})\prod_{k=1}^{t-1}p(r_{k}|s_{1},a_{k})}.

We next show that eq. (
S.96
) follows from eq. (
S.97
) by induction with respect to
t
.

Base case.
For
t=2
, we have from eq. (
S.97
).

p(s_{1}|a_{1},r_{1})\propto p(s_{1})p(r_{1}|s_{1},a_{1})=\mbox{Beta}\left(s_{1};(1,1)\right)\mbox{B}(r_{1};s_{1})^{a_{1}}\mbox{B}(r_{1};1-s_{1})^{1-a_{1}}

(S.98)

Assume first that
a_{1}=0
. Then

\displaystyle\begin{split}p(s_{1}|a_{1}=0,r_{1})&\propto\mbox{Beta}\left(s_{1};(1,1)\right)\mbox{B}(r_{1};s_{1})^{0}\mbox{B}(r_{1};1-s_{1})^{1-0}\\
&=\mbox{Beta}\left(s_{1};(1,1)\right)\mbox{B}(r_{1};1-s_{1})\\
&=(s_{1})^{1-1}(1-s_{1})^{1-1}(1-s_{1})^{r_{1}}(s_{1})^{1-r_{1}}\\
&=(s_{1})^{1+1-r_{1}-1}(1-s_{1})^{1+r_{1}-1}.\\
\end{split}

(S.99)

Assume now that
a_{1}=1
. Then, analogously

\displaystyle\begin{split}p(s_{1}|a_{1}=1,r_{1})&\propto\mbox{Beta}\left(s_{1};(1,1)\right)\mbox{B}(r_{1};s_{1})^{1}\mbox{B}(r_{1};1-s_{1})^{1-1}\\
&=\mbox{Beta}\left(s_{1};(1,1)\right)\mbox{B}(r_{1};s_{1})\\
&=(s_{1})^{1-1}(1-s_{1})^{1-1}(s_{1})^{r_{1}}(1-s_{1})^{1-r_{1}}\\
&=(s_{1})^{1+r_{1}-1}(1-s_{1})^{1+1-r_{1}-1}.\\
\end{split}

(S.100)

We thus observe the following update rules for the parameters of the beta distribution:

•

If
a_{1}=0
, then
\alpha_{2}^{(1)}=1+1-r_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}^{(2)}=1+r_{1}
.

•

If
a_{1}=1
, then
\alpha_{2}^{(1)}=1+r_{1}\hskip 9.24994pt\,\,\,\,
and
\alpha_{2}^{(2)}=1+1-r_{1}
.

For
\alpha^{(1)}_{2}
, we thus have the update rule

\alpha_{2}^{(1)}=1+\begin{cases}1&\mbox{ for }a_{1}=0\mbox{ and }r_{1}=0\\
0&\mbox{ for }a_{1}=0\mbox{ and }r_{1}=1\\
0&\mbox{ for }a_{1}=1\mbox{ and }r_{1}=0\\
1&\mbox{ for }a_{1}=1\mbox{ and }r_{1}=1\\
\end{cases}

(S.101)

and for
\alpha^{(2)}_{2}
, we have the update rule

\alpha_{2}^{(2)}=1+\begin{cases}0&\mbox{ for }a_{1}=0\mbox{ and }r_{1}=0\\
1&\mbox{ for }a_{1}=0\mbox{ and }r_{1}=1\\
1&\mbox{ for }a_{1}=1\mbox{ and }r_{1}=0\\
0&\mbox{ for }a_{1}=1\mbox{ and }r_{1}=1\\
\end{cases}

(S.102)

With

\tilde{r}_{t}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}\left(1+(-1)^{0+0}\right)=1&\mbox{ for }a_{1}=0\mbox{ and }r_{1}=0\\
\frac{1}{2}\left(1+(-1)^{0+1}\right)=0&\mbox{ for }a_{1}=0\mbox{ and }r_{1}=1\\
\frac{1}{2}\left(1+(-1)^{1+0}\right)=0&\mbox{ for }a_{1}=1\mbox{ and }r_{1}=0\\
\frac{1}{2}\left(1+(-1)^{1+1}\right)=1&\mbox{ for }a_{1}=1\mbox{ and }r_{1}=1\\
\end{cases}

(S.103)

and the parametric form of the Beta distribution, it thus follows that

p(s_{1}|a_{1},r_{1})=\mbox{Beta}(s_{1};\alpha_{2})

(S.104)

with

\displaystyle\begin{split}\alpha_{2}&=\left(1+\tilde{r}_{1},1+(1-\tilde{r}_{1})\right)\\
&=\left(1+\tilde{r}_{1},2-\tilde{r}_{1}\right)\\
&=\left(1+\sum_{k=1}^{1}\tilde{r}_{k},t-\sum_{k=1}^{1}\tilde{r}_{k}\right)\\
&=\left(1+\sum_{k=1}^{t-1}\tilde{r}_{k},t-\sum_{k=1}^{t-1}\tilde{r}_{k}\right).\end{split}

(S.105)

Thus, eq. (
S.96
) holds for
t=2
.

Induction step.

Assume now that

p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-2},r_{1:t-2})=\mbox{Beta}(s_{1};\alpha_{t-1})\mbox{ for some }t=3,...,T.

(S.106)

Then from eq. (
S.97
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})&\propto p(s_{1})\prod_{k=1}^{t-1}p(r_{k}|s_{1},a_{k})\\
&=\left(p(s_{1})\prod_{k=1}^{t-2}p(r_{k}|s_{1},a_{k})\right)p(r_{t-1}|s_{1},a_{t-1})\\
&\propto p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-2},r_{1:t-2})p(r_{t-1}|s_{1},a_{t-1})\\
&=\mbox{Beta}(s_{1};\alpha_{t-1})\mbox{B}(r_{t-1};s_{1})^{a_{t-1}}\mbox{B}(r_{t-1};1-s_{1})^{1-a_{t-1}}\end{split}

(S.107)

Assume first that
a_{t-1}=0
. Then

\displaystyle\begin{split}p(s_{1}|a_{t-1}=0,r_{t-1})&\propto\mbox{Beta}\left(s_{1};\left(\alpha^{(1)}_{t-1},\alpha^{(2)}_{t-1}\right)\right)\mbox{B}(r_{t-1};s_{1})^{0}\mbox{B}(r_{t-1};1-s_{1})^{1-0}\\
&=\mbox{Beta}\left(s_{1};\left(\alpha^{(1)}_{t-1},\alpha^{(2)}_{t-1}\right)\right)\mbox{B}(r_{t-1};1-s_{1})\\
&=(s_{1})^{\alpha^{(1)}_{t-1}-1}(1-s_{1})^{\alpha^{(2)}_{t-1}-1}(1-s_{1})^{r_{t-1}}(s_{1})^{1-r_{t-1}}\\
&=(s_{1})^{\alpha^{(1)}_{t-1}+1-r_{t-1}-1}(1-s_{1})^{\alpha^{(2)}_{t-1}+r_{t-1}-1}.\\
\end{split}

(S.108)

Assume now that
a_{1}=1
. Then, analogously

\displaystyle\begin{split}p(s_{1}|a_{t-1}=1,r_{t-1})&\propto\mbox{Beta}\left(s_{1};\left(\alpha^{(1)}_{t-1},\alpha^{(2)}_{t-1}\right)\right)\mbox{B}(r_{t-1};s_{1})^{1}\mbox{B}(r_{t-1};1-s_{1})^{1-1}\\
&=\mbox{Beta}\left(s_{1};\left(\alpha^{(1)}_{t-1},\alpha^{(2)}_{t-1}\right)\right)\mbox{B}(r_{t-1};s_{1})\\
&=(s_{1})^{\alpha^{(1)}_{t-1}-1}(1-s_{1})^{\alpha^{(2)}_{t-1}-1}(s_{1})^{r_{t-1}}(1-s_{1})^{1-r_{t-1}}\\
&=(s_{1})^{\alpha^{(1)}_{t-1}+r_{t-1}-1}(1-s_{1})^{\alpha^{(2)}_{t-1}+1-r_{t-1}-1}.\\
\end{split}

(S.109)

We thus observe the following update rules for the parameters of the Beta distribution:

•

If
a_{t-1}=0
, then
\alpha^{(1)}_{t}=\alpha^{(1)}_{t-1}+1-r_{t-1}
and
\alpha_{t}^{(2)}=\alpha^{(2)}_{t-1}+r_{t-1}
.

•

If
a_{t-1}=1
, then
\alpha^{(1)}_{t}=\alpha^{(1)}_{t-1}+r_{t-1}\hskip 9.24994pt\,\,\,\,
and
\alpha_{t}^{(2)}=\alpha^{(2)}_{t-1}+1-r_{t-1}
.

For
\alpha^{(1)}_{t}
, we thus have the update rule

\alpha^{(1)}_{t}=\alpha^{(1)}_{t-1}+\begin{cases}1&\mbox{ for }a_{t-1}=0\mbox{ and }r_{t-1}=0\\
0&\mbox{ for }a_{t-1}=0\mbox{ and }r_{t-1}=1\\
0&\mbox{ for }a_{t-1}=1\mbox{ and }r_{t-1}=0\\
1&\mbox{ for }a_{t-1}=1\mbox{ and }r_{t-1}=1\\
\end{cases}

(S.110)

and for
\alpha^{(2)}_{t}
, we have the update rule

\alpha^{(2)}_{t}=\alpha^{(2)}_{t-1}+\begin{cases}0&\mbox{ for }a_{t-1}=0\mbox{ and }r_{t-1}=0\\
1&\mbox{ for }a_{t-1}=0\mbox{ and }r_{t-1}=1\\
1&\mbox{ for }a_{t-1}=1\mbox{ and }r_{t-1}=0\\
0&\mbox{ for }a_{t-1}=1\mbox{ and }r_{t-1}=1\\
\end{cases}.

(S.111)

With

\tilde{r}_{t-1}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}\left(1+(-1)^{0+0}\right)=1&\mbox{ for }a_{t-1}=0\mbox{ and }r_{t-1}=0\\
\frac{1}{2}\left(1+(-1)^{0+1}\right)=0&\mbox{ for }a_{t-1}=0\mbox{ and }r_{t-1}=1\\
\frac{1}{2}\left(1+(-1)^{1+0}\right)=0&\mbox{ for }a_{t-1}=1\mbox{ and }r_{t-1}=0\\
\frac{1}{2}\left(1+(-1)^{1+1}\right)=1&\mbox{ for }a_{t-1}=1\mbox{ and }r_{t-1}=1\\
\end{cases}

(S.112)

and the parametric form of the Beta distribution, it thus follows that

p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})=\mbox{Beta}(s_{1};\alpha_{t})

(S.113)

with

\displaystyle\begin{split}\alpha_{t}&=\left(\alpha^{(1)}_{t-1}+\tilde{r}_{t-1},\alpha^{(2)}_{t-1}+1-\tilde{r}_{t-1}\right)\\
&=\left(1+\sum_{k=1}^{t-2}\tilde{r}_{k}+\tilde{r}_{t-1},t-1-\sum_{k=1}^{t-2}\tilde{r}_{k}+1-\tilde{r}_{t-1}\right)\\
&=\left(1+\sum_{k=1}^{t-1}\tilde{r}_{k},t-\sum_{k=1}^{t-1}\tilde{r}_{k}\right)\end{split}

(S.114)

Notably,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\alpha_{t}&=\left(\alpha^{(1)}_{t-1}+\tilde{r}_{t-1},\alpha^{(2)}_{t-1}+1-\tilde{r}_{t-1}\right)\\
&=\left(\alpha_{t-1}^{(1)}+\frac{1}{2}\left(1+(-1)^{r_{t-1}+a_{t-1}}\right),\alpha_{t-1}^{(2)}+1-\frac{1}{2}\left(1+(-1)^{r_{t-1}+a_{t-1}}\right)\right)\end{split}

(S.115)

implies the Markovian belief state update as formulated in the belief state update function eq. (
29
), because
\alpha_{t-1}=\left(\alpha_{t-1}^{(1)},\alpha_{t-1}^{(2)}\right)
corresponds to the parametric representation of the previous belief state
b_{t-1}
. For later reference, we also note that eq. (
S.115
) implies that

•

For
\tilde{r}_{t}=0

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}(s_{1})&=\frac{\alpha_{t}^{(1)}}{\alpha_{t}^{(1)}+\alpha_{t}^{(2)}}\\
&=\frac{\alpha_{t-1}^{(1)}+0}{\alpha_{t-1}^{(1)}+0+\alpha_{t-1}^{(2)}+1-0}\\
&=\frac{\alpha_{t-1}^{(1)}}{\alpha_{t-1}^{(1)}+\alpha_{t-1}^{(2)}+1}\end{split}

(S.116)

•

For
\tilde{r}_{t}=1

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}(s_{1})&=\frac{\alpha_{t}^{(1)}}{\alpha_{t}^{(1)}+\alpha_{t}^{(2)}}\\
&=\frac{\alpha_{t-1}^{(1)}+1}{\alpha_{t-1}^{(1)}+1+\alpha_{t-1}^{(2)}+1-1}\\
&=\frac{\alpha_{t-1}^{(1)}+1}{\alpha_{t-1}^{(1)}+1+\alpha_{t-1}^{(2)}}\end{split}

(S.117)

∎

Theorem S.7

(Decision optimality)
.

Let

p(s_{1},d_{1:T},a_{1:T},r_{1:T})\\
=p(s_{1})p(d_{1})p(a_{1}|d_{1})p(r_{1}|s_{1},d_{1})\prod_{t=2}^{T}p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(a_{t}|d_{t})p(r_{t}|s_{1},d_{t})

(S.118)

denote the agent model induced by eq. (
24
), eq. (
40
) and eq. (
41
) under
the assumption of the absence of post-decision noise, i.e., with

p(r_{t}|s_{1},d_{t}):=\mbox{B}(r_{t};s_{1})^{d_{t}}\mbox{B}(r_{t};1-s_{1})^{1-d_{t}}.

(S.119)

Let
\varphi
denote a history-dependent policy, i.e., a function of the form
(
75
;
104
, cf.)

\varphi:A^{t-1}\times R^{t-1}\to D,(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})\mapsto\varphi(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})=:d_{t}

(S.120)

and let
\Pi
denote the set of all such policies in the current scenario. Then the policy

\varphi^{*}:=\delta\circ\phi\circ\beta

(S.121)

maximizes the agent’s cumulative expected reward, i.e.,

\varphi^{*}=\argmax_{\varphi\in\Pi}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}\left(\mathbb{E}_{p(r_{t}|s_{1},d_{t})}(r_{t})\right)\right)\mbox{ for }t=1,...,T.

(S.122)

Proof.

We first note that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{p(r_{t}|s_{1},d_{1})}(r_{t})&=0\cdot\mbox{B}(0;s_{1})^{d_{t}}\mbox{B}(0;1-s_{1})^{1-d_{t}}+1\cdot\mbox{B}(1;s_{1})^{d_{t}}\mbox{B}(1;1-s_{1})^{1-d_{t}}\\
&=\mbox{B}(1;s_{1})^{d_{t}}\mbox{B}(1;1-s_{1})^{1-d_{t}}\\
&=(s_{1})^{d_{t}}(1-s_{1})^{1-d_{t}}\end{split}

(S.123)

Substituting
\varphi(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})
for
d_{t}
, we thus consider the maximization problem

\max_{\varphi\in\Pi}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}\left((s_{1})^{\varphi(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}(1-s_{1})^{1-\varphi(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}\right)\right)

(S.124)

Given the additive separability of the objective function, this maximization is equivalent to the
T
maximization problems
(cf.
95
, Section 7.4)
for
t=1,...,T
,

\max_{\varphi\in\Pi}\mathbb{E}_{p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}\left((s_{1})^{\varphi(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}(1-s_{1})^{1-\varphi(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}\right)

(S.125)

We are thus concerned with identifying a function
\varphi
for which
\varphi(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})\in\{0,1\}
and which maximizes the posterior expected value of

(s_{1})^{\varphi(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}(1-s_{1})^{1-\varphi(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}
. We are thus led to consider

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\max_{\varphi(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})\in\{0,1\}}\mathbb{E}_{p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}\left((s_{1})^{\varphi(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}(1-s_{1})^{1-\varphi(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}\right)\\
&=\max\left\{\mathbb{E}_{p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}\left((s_{1})^{0}(1-s_{1})^{1-0}\right),\mathbb{E}_{p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}\left((s_{1})^{1}(1-s_{1})^{1-1}\right)\right\}\\
&=\max\left\{\mathbb{E}_{p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}\left(1-s_{1}\right),\mathbb{E}_{p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}\left(s_{1}\right)\right\}\\
&=\max\left\{1-\mathbb{E}_{p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}\left(s_{1}\right),\mathbb{E}_{p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}\left(s_{1}\right)\right\}\\
&=\max\left\{1-\frac{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}}{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+{\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}},\frac{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}}{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+{\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}}\right\}\end{split}

(S.126)

with

\displaystyle\begin{split}\argmax_{\varphi(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})\in\{0,1\}}\mathbb{E}_{p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}&\left((s_{1})^{\varphi(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}(1-s_{1})^{1-\varphi(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}\right)=0\\
\Leftrightarrow 1-\frac{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}}{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+{\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}}&\geq\frac{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}}{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+{\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}}\\
\Leftrightarrow\frac{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+{\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}}{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+{\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}}-\frac{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}}{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+{\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}}&\geq\frac{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}}{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+{\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}}\\
\Leftrightarrow\frac{\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+{\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}}&\geq\frac{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}}{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+{\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}}\\
\Leftrightarrow\alpha^{(1)}_{t}&\leq\alpha^{(2)}_{t}\end{split}

(S.127)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\argmax_{\varphi(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})\in\{0,1\}}\mathbb{E}_{p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}&\left((s_{1})^{\varphi(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}(1-s_{1})^{1-\varphi(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}\right)=1\\
\Leftrightarrow 1-\frac{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}}{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+{\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}}&<\frac{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}}{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+{\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}}\\
\Leftrightarrow\frac{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+{\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}}{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+{\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}}-\frac{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}}{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+{\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}}&<\frac{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}}{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+{\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}}\\
\Leftrightarrow\frac{\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+{\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}}&<\frac{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}}{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+{\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}}\\
\Leftrightarrow\alpha^{(1)}_{t}&>\alpha^{(2)}_{t}\end{split}

(S.128)

Such a
\varphi
is thus of the form

\varphi:A^{t-1}\times R^{t-1}\to D,(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})\mapsto\varphi(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1}):=\begin{cases}0&\mbox{ if }\alpha^{(1)}_{t}\leq\alpha^{(2)}_{t}\\
1&\mbox{ if }\alpha^{(1)}_{t}>\alpha^{(2)}_{t}\\
\end{cases}

(S.129)

But as shown in
Theorem S.8
this is exactly the functional form of
\delta\circ\phi\circ\beta
,
because

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\delta\circ\phi\circ\beta)(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})=0\Leftrightarrow p(d_{t}=0|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})=1\Leftrightarrow\alpha^{(1)}_{t}\leq\alpha^{(2)}_{t}\\
(\delta\circ\phi\circ\beta)(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})=1\Leftrightarrow p(d_{t}=1|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})=1\Leftrightarrow\alpha^{(1)}_{t}>\alpha^{(2)}_{t}\end{split}

(S.130)

and thus

\delta\circ\phi\circ\beta=\varphi^{*}=\argmax_{\varphi\in\Pi}\left(\mathbb{E}_{p(s_{1}|a_{1:T-1},r_{1:T-1})}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{p(r_{t}|s_{1},d_{1})}(r_{t})\right)\right).

(S.131)

∎

Theorem S.8

(Conditional decision distribution)
.

Given the agent model
\mathcal{A}
defined in eq. (
27
), the distribution of an agent’s decision

d_{t}=(\delta\circ\phi\circ\beta)(b_{t-1},a_{t-1},r_{t-1})

(S.132)

given
b_{t-1},a_{t-1}
and
r_{t-1}
can be written as

p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})=\left\llbracket\alpha^{(1)}_{t}\leq\alpha^{(2)}_{t}\right\rrbracket^{1-d_{t}}\left\llbracket\alpha^{(1)}_{t}>\alpha^{(2)}_{t}\right\rrbracket^{d_{t}},

(S.133)

where

\alpha^{(1)}_{t}:=1+\sum_{k=1}^{t-1}\tilde{r}_{k}\mbox{ and }\alpha^{(2)}_{t}:=t-\sum_{k=1}^{t-1}\tilde{r}_{k}.

(S.134)

Proof.

We first note that because
d_{t}
is a function of
a_{1:t-1}
and
r_{1:t-1}
,
p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})
is degenerate, taking on the value
1
,
if
d_{t}=(\delta\circ\phi\circ\beta)(a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})
and
0
else. Next, because
d_{t}
takes on values in
D=\{0,1\}
it suffices to consider
p(d_{t}=0|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})
, as
p(d_{t}=1|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})=1-p(d_{t}=0|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})
follows immediately. We hence assume that
d_{t}=0
and show that this is equivalent to
\alpha^{(1)}_{t}\leq\alpha^{(2)}_{t}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}d_{t}&=0\\
\Leftrightarrow v_{t}^{0}&\geq v_{t}^{1}\\
\Leftrightarrow 1-\mathbb{E}_{p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}(s_{1})&\geq\mathbb{E}_{p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}(s_{1})\\
\Leftrightarrow 1-\frac{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}}{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}&\geq\frac{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}}{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}\\
\Leftrightarrow\frac{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}-\frac{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}}{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}&\geq\frac{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}}{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}\\
\Leftrightarrow\frac{\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}&\geq\frac{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}}{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}+\alpha^{(2)}_{t}}\\
\Leftrightarrow\alpha^{(2)}_{t}&\geq\alpha^{(1)}_{t}\\
\Leftrightarrow\alpha^{(1)}_{t}&\leq\alpha^{(2)}_{t}\end{split}

(S.135)

Casting

p(d_{t}=0|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})=1
, if
\alpha^{(2)}_{t}\leq\alpha^{(1)}_{t}
, and

p(d_{t}=0|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})=0
, if
\alpha^{(2)}_{t}>\alpha^{(1)}_{t}
, implies that

p(d_{t}=1|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})=0
, if
\alpha^{(1)}_{t}\leq\alpha^{(2)}_{t}
, and

p(d_{t}=1|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})=1
, if
\alpha^{(1)}_{t}>\alpha^{(2)}_{t}
, and thus allows
for writing the conditional distribution of
d_{t}
given
a_{1:t-1}
and
r_{1:t-1}
as

p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})=\left\llbracket\alpha^{(1)}_{t}\leq\alpha^{(2)}_{t}\right\rrbracket^{1-d_{t}}\left\llbracket\alpha^{(1)}_{t}>\alpha^{(2)}_{t}\right\rrbracket^{d_{t}}

(S.136)

∎

Theorem S.9

(Conditional log-likelihood function)
.

Let

p(s_{1},d_{1:T},a_{1:T},r_{1:T})\\
=p(s_{1})p(d_{1})p(a_{1}|d_{1})p(r_{1}|s_{1},a_{1})\prod_{t=2}^{T}p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(a_{t}|d_{t})p(r_{t}|s_{1},a_{t})

(S.137)

denote the ABM induced by eqs. eq. (
24
), eq. (
40
), and
eq.~\eqref{eq:D-2}
. Then

p(a_{1:T}|r_{1:T})=\prod_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{d_{t}}p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(a_{t}|d_{t})

(S.138)

and

\sum_{d_{t}}p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(a_{t}|d_{t})=\left\llbracket\alpha^{(1)}_{t}\leq\alpha^{(2)}_{t}\right\rrbracket\mbox{B}(a_{t};\tau)+\left\llbracket\alpha^{(1)}_{t}>\alpha^{(2)}_{t}\right\rrbracket\mbox{B}(a_{t};1-\tau).

(S.139)

Proof.

With respect to eq. (
S.138
), we have

\displaystyle p(a_{1:T}|r_{1:T})

\displaystyle=\frac{p(a_{1:T},r_{1:T})}{p(r_{1:T})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\prod_{t=1}^{T}p(a_{t},r_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}{p(r_{1:T})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\prod_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{d_{t}}\int_{s_{1}}p(s_{1},d_{t},a_{t},r_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}{p(r_{1:T})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\prod_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{d_{t}}\int_{s_{1}}p(r_{t}|s_{1},d_{t},a_{t},a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(s_{1},d_{t},a_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}{p(r_{1:T})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\prod_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{d_{t}}\int_{s_{1}}p(r_{t}|s_{1},d_{t},a_{t},a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(a_{t}|s_{1},d_{t},a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(s_{1},d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}{p(r_{1:T})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\prod_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{d_{t}}\int_{s_{1}}p(r_{t}|s_{1},d_{t},a_{t},a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(a_{t}|s_{1},d_{t},a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(d_{t}|s_{1},a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}{p(r_{1:T})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\prod_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{d_{t}}\int_{s_{1}}p(r_{t}|s_{1},a_{t})p(a_{t}|d_{t})p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(s_{1})}{p(r_{1:T})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\prod_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{d_{t}}p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(a_{t}|d_{t})\int_{s_{1}}p(s_{1})p(r_{t}|s_{1},a_{t})}{p(r_{1:T})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\prod_{t=1}^{T}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{d_{t}}p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(a_{t}|d_{t})}{p(r_{1:T})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{1}{2^{T}}\prod_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{d_{t}}p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(a_{t}|d_{t})}{p(r_{1:T})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{1}{2^{T}}\prod_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{d_{t}}p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(a_{t}|d_{t})}{\sum_{a_{1:T}p(a_{1:T},r_{1:T})}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{1}{2^{T}}\prod_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{d_{t}}p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(a_{t}|d_{t})}{\sum_{a_{1:T}}\frac{1}{2^{T}}\prod_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{d_{t}}p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(a_{t}|d_{t})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{1}{2^{T}}\prod_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{d_{t}}p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(a_{t}|d_{t})}{\frac{1}{2^{T}}\prod_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{a_{t}}\sum_{d_{t}}p(a_{t},d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\prod_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{d_{t}}p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(a_{t}|d_{t})}{\prod_{t=1}^{T}1}

\displaystyle=\prod_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{d_{t}}p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(a_{t}|d_{t})

For the 9th equality, we have used that the integral with respect to
s_{1}
evaluates to
\frac{1}{2}
irrespective of the values of
a_{t}
and
r_{t}
, as

•

for
a_{t}=0
, we have

\int_{s_{1}}p(s_{1})p(r_{t}|s_{1},a_{t}=0)=\int_{0}^{1}1\cdot\mbox{B}(r_{t};1-s_{1})\,ds_{1}=\int_{0}^{1}(1-s_{1})^{r_{t}}s_{1}^{1-r_{t}}\,ds_{1},

(S.140)

which

\circ

for
r_{t}=0
evaluates to
\int_{0}^{1}s_{1}\,ds_{1}=\left(\frac{1}{2}s_{1}^{2}\right)\big|_{0}^{1}=\frac{1}{2}-0=\frac{1}{2}
,

\circ

for
r_{t}=1
evaluates to
\int_{0}^{1}(1-s_{1})\,ds_{1}=\left(s_{1}-\frac{1}{2}s_{1}^{2}\right)|_{0}^{1}=1-\frac{1}{2}-0+0=\frac{1}{2}
,

•

for
a_{t}=1
, we have

\int_{s_{1}}p(s_{1})p(r_{t}|s_{1},a_{t}=1)=\int_{0}^{1}1\cdot\mbox{B}(r_{t}s_{1})\,ds_{1}=\int_{0}^{1}s_{1}^{r_{t}}(1-s_{1})^{1-r_{t}}\,ds_{1},

(S.141)

which

\circ

for
r_{t}=0
evaluates to
\int_{0}^{1}(1-s_{1})\,ds_{1}=\left(s_{1}-\frac{1}{2}s_{1}^{2}\right)|_{0}^{1}=1-\frac{1}{2}-0+0=\frac{1}{2}
,

\circ

for
r_{t}=1
evaluates to
\int_{0}^{1}s_{1}\,ds_{1}=\left(\frac{1}{2}s_{1}^{2}\right)\big|_{0}^{1}=\frac{1}{2}-0=\frac{1}{2}
.

Finally, with respect to eq. (
S.139
), we have with
Theorem S.8
and eq. (
41
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{d_{t}}p(d_{t}|&a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(a_{t}|d_{t})\\
&=\sum_{d_{t}}\left\llbracket\alpha^{(1)}_{t}\leq\alpha^{(2)}_{t}\right\rrbracket^{1-d_{t}}\left\llbracket\alpha^{(1)}_{t}>\alpha^{(2)}_{t}\right\rrbracket^{d_{t}}\mbox{B}(a_{t};1-\tau)^{d_{t}}\mbox{B}(a_{t};\tau)^{1-d_{t}}\\
&=\left\llbracket\alpha^{(1)}_{t}\leq\alpha^{(2)}_{t}\right\rrbracket\mbox{B}(a_{t};\tau)+\left\llbracket\alpha^{(1)}_{t}>\alpha^{(2)}_{t}\right\rrbracket\mbox{B}(a_{t};1-\tau)\end{split}

(S.142)

∎

Figure S.19:
Sampling-based validations of
Theorem S.8
and
Theorem S.9
. (A) Histogram-based estimation of
p(d_{t}=1|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})
parameterized by
\alpha_{t}=(\smash{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}},\smash{\alpha^{(2)}_{t}})
as generated during model validation as a gray transparent surface. Because
p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})
is a degenerate distribution, its estimate is identical to the analytical result of
Theorem S.8
. Note that
(\smash{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}},\smash{\alpha^{(2)}_{t}})
combinations beyond the main diagonal are inadmissible given the experimental constraints of 30 trials per block and are thus not associated with conditional decision probabilities. (B) Histogram-based estimation of
p(a_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})
based on simulated joint observations of
a_{t}
and
\alpha_{t}
as generated during model validation for two settings of the post-decision noise parameter
\tau
. Each subpanel shows the histogram-based estimate of
p(a_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})
as a gray wrinkled surface, reflecting the noise in its estimation, and the analytical result of
p(a_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})
according to
Theorem S.8
as a blue transparent surface. As for (A), the
(\smash{\alpha^{(1)}_{t}},\smash{\alpha^{(2)}_{t}})
combinations behind the main diagonal are inadmissible in the current experimental context and thus not associated with a conditional action probability. (C) Exemplary conditional log-likelihood functions based on
Theorem S.9
. These conditional log-likelihood functions are based on the joint observations of actions and rewards for a single simulated participant dataset (10 blocks with 30 trials each) with the true, but unknown, parameter values indicated by the lines’ shade of red and documented in the legend. For this one-dimensional parameter space, the conditional log-likelihood functions exhibit clear maxima, which are marked by dots. The continuous curved nature of these one-dimensional conditional log-likelihood functions suggests that the data generated under the current experimental conditions is informative about the post-decision noise parameter under the assumptions of model A1. For full analytical details, please refer to
abm_figure_S8.py.

Theorem S.10

(Beta-Bernoulli and Rescorla-Wagner learning equivalence)
.

Let
\phi
and
\varphi
denote the decision value functions of the Beta-Bernoulli agent model eq. (
27
) and the
adaptive learning rate Rescorla-Wagner agent model eq. (
43
) for the symmetric bandit task. Then

\phi(b_{t})=\varphi(\hat{s}_{t})\mbox{ for all }a_{1:t-1}\mbox{ and }r_{1:t-1}

(S.143)

and thus, given identical action and reward observation histories, both make identical decisions.

Proof.

We first note that with eq. (
34
) and eq. (
45
)

\phi(b_{t})=\varphi(\hat{s}_{t})\mbox{ for all }a_{1:t-1}\mbox{ and }r_{1:t-1}

(S.144)

is equivalent to

\mathbb{E}_{p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}(s_{1})=\hat{s}_{t}\mbox{ for all }a_{1:t-1}\mbox{ and }r_{1:t-1}

(S.145)

and thus showing that the adaptive learning rate Rescorla-Wagner update function eq. (
44
) is equivalent to the posterior expected value update implied by the belief state update function of eq. (
29
). We show eq. (
S.145
) by induction with respect to
t

Base case

Let
t=1
. Then with
\hat{s}_{1}=0.5
by definition and from eq. (
33
),

\mathbb{E}_{p(s_{1}|a_{1:0},r_{1:0})}(s_{1})=\frac{\alpha_{1}^{(1)}}{\alpha_{1}^{(1)}+\alpha_{1}^{(2)}}=\frac{1}{1+1}=0.5

(S.146)

Induction step

Assume that

\hat{s}_{t-1}=\mathbb{E}_{p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-2},r_{1:t-2})}(s_{1})=\frac{\alpha_{t-1}^{(1)}}{\alpha_{t-1}^{(1)}+\alpha_{t-1}^{(2)}}

(S.147)

for some
t=2,3,...T
(the base case shows that this assumption is justified for
t=2
). For ease of notation, define

a:=\alpha_{t-1}^{(1)}\mbox{ and }b:=\alpha_{t-1}^{(2)}

(S.148)

and thus

\hat{s}_{t-1}=\frac{a}{a+b}\mbox{ and }t=a+b

(S.149)

We consider the cases

\tilde{r}_{t-1}=0\Leftrightarrow a_{t-1}=0,r_{t-1}=1\lor a_{t-1}=1,r_{t-1}=0

(S.150)

and

\tilde{r}_{t-1}=1\Leftrightarrow a_{t-1}=0,r_{t-1}=0\lor a_{t-1}=1,r_{t-1}=1

(S.151)

in turn.

(1) Let
\tilde{r}_{t-1}=0
.Then

\displaystyle\hat{s}_{t}

\displaystyle=\hat{s}_{t-1}+\frac{1}{t+1}\left(\tilde{r}_{t-1}-\hat{s}_{t-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{a+b}+\frac{1}{a+b+1}\left(0-\frac{a}{a+b}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{a+b}-\frac{a}{(a+b+1)(a+b)}

\displaystyle=\frac{a(a+b+1)}{(a+b+1)(a+b)}-\frac{a}{(a+b+1)(a+b)}

\displaystyle=\frac{a(a+b+1)-a}{(a+b+1)(a+b)}

\displaystyle=\frac{a^{2}+ab+a-a}{(a+b+1)(a+b)}

\displaystyle=\frac{a^{2}+ab}{(a+b+1)(a+b)}

\displaystyle=\frac{a(a+b)}{(a+b+1)(a+b)}

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{a+b+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{a+b+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{t-1}^{(1)}}{\alpha_{t-1}^{(1)}+\alpha_{t-1}^{(2)}+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}(s_{1}),

where the last equality corresponds to eq. (
S.116
).

(1) Let
\tilde{r}_{t-1}=1
.Then

\displaystyle\hat{s}_{t}

\displaystyle=\hat{s}_{t-1}+\frac{1}{t+1}\left(\tilde{r}_{t-1}-\hat{s}_{t-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{a+b}+\frac{1}{a+b+1}\left(1-\frac{a}{a+b}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{a+b}+\frac{1}{a+b+1}-\frac{a}{(a+b+1)(a+b)}

\displaystyle=\frac{a(a+b+1)}{(a+b+1)(a+b)}+\frac{a+b}{(a+b+1)(a+b)}-\frac{a}{(a+b+1)(a+b)}

\displaystyle=\frac{a(a+b+1)+b}{(a+b+1)(a+b)}

\displaystyle=\frac{a^{2}+ab+a+b}{(a+b+1)(a+b)}

\displaystyle=\frac{(a+b)(a+1)}{(a+b+1)(a+b)}

\displaystyle=\frac{a+1}{a+1+b}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{t-1}^{(1)}+1}{\alpha_{t-1}^{(1)}+1+\alpha_{t-1}^{(2)}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{p(s_{1}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})}(s_{1}),

where the last equality corresponds to eq. (
S.117
).
∎

We validated the equivalence of the agents’ decision value functions numerically in
abm_equivalence.py
, and, as expected, found no numerical differences.

S9.3
Rescorla-Wagner agent model analysis

Theorem S.11

(Conditional decision distribution)
.

Given the agent model
\mathcal{A}
defined in eq. (
46
), the distribution of an agent’s decision

d_{t}=(\delta\circ\phi\circ\beta)(\hat{s}_{t-1},a_{t-1},r_{t-1})

(S.152)

given
\hat{s}_{t-1},a_{t-1}
and
r_{t-1}
can be written as

p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})=\left\llbracket\hat{s}_{t}\leq 1-\hat{s}_{t}\right\rrbracket^{1-d_{t}}\left\llbracket\hat{s}_{t}>1-\hat{s}_{t}\right\rrbracket^{d_{t}},

(S.153)

where

\hat{s}_{t}=(1-\lambda)^{t-1}\hat{s}_{1}+\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{t-1}(1-\lambda)^{t-1-j}\tilde{r}_{j}.

(S.154)

Proof.

We first note that because
d_{t}
is a function of
a_{1:t-1}
and
r_{1:t-1}
,
p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})
is degenerate, taking on the value
1
,
if
d_{t}=(\delta\circ\varphi\circ\psi)(\hat{s}_{t-1},a_{t-1},r_{t-1})
and
0
else. Next, because
d_{t}
takes on values in
D=\{0,1\}
it suffices to consider
p(d_{t}=0|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})
, as
p(d_{t}=1|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})=1-p(d_{t}=0|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})
follows immediately. We hence assume that
d_{t}=0
and show that this is equivalent to
\hat{s}_{t}\leq 1-\hat{s}_{t}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}d_{t}&=0\\
\Leftrightarrow v_{t}^{0}&\geq v_{t}^{1}\\
\Leftrightarrow 1-\hat{s}_{t}&\geq\hat{s}_{t}\\
\Leftrightarrow\hat{s}_{t}&\leq 1-\hat{s}_{t}\end{split}

(S.155)

Casting

p(d_{t}=0|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})=1
, if
\hat{s}_{t}\leq 1-\hat{s}_{t}
, and

p(d_{t}=0|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})=0
, if
\hat{s}_{t}>1-\hat{s}_{t}
, implies that

p(d_{t}=1|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})=0
, if
\hat{s}_{t}>1-\hat{s}_{t}
, and

p(d_{t}=1|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})=1
, if
\hat{s}_{t}\leq 1-\hat{s}_{t}
, and thus allows
for writing the conditional distribution of
d_{t}
given
a_{1:t-1}
and
r_{1:t-1}
as

p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})=\left\llbracket\hat{s}_{t}\leq 1-\hat{s}_{t}\right\rrbracket^{1-d_{t}}\left\llbracket\hat{s}_{t}>1-\hat{s}_{t}\right\rrbracket^{d_{t}}.

(S.156)

Finally, we validate the closed-form solution eq. (
S.154
)
of the probability state estimate
\hat{s}_{t}
of the Rescorla-Wagner update equation by means of induction
with respect to
t
.

Base case

Let
t=2
. Then, according to eq. (
47
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{s}_{2}&=\hat{s}_{1}+\lambda(\tilde{r}_{1}-\hat{s}_{1})\\
&=\hat{s}_{1}-\lambda\hat{s}_{1}+\lambda\tilde{r}_{1}\\
&=(1-\lambda)\hat{s}_{1}+\lambda(1-\lambda)^{0}\tilde{r}_{1}\\
&=(1-\lambda)\hat{s}_{1}+\lambda(1-\lambda)^{2-1-1}\tilde{r}_{1}\\
&=(1-\lambda)\hat{s}_{1}+\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{1}(1-\lambda)^{2-1-j}\tilde{r}_{j}\\
&=(1-\lambda)^{2-1}\hat{s}_{1}+\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{2-1}(1-\lambda)^{2-1-j}\tilde{r}_{j}\\
&=(1-\lambda)^{t-1}\hat{s}_{1}+\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{t-1}(1-\lambda)^{t-1-j}\tilde{r}_{j}\\
\end{split}

(S.157)

Induction step

Assume that

\hat{s}_{t-1}=(1-\lambda)^{t-2}\hat{s}_{1}+\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{t-2}(1-\lambda)^{t-2-j}\tilde{r}_{j}

(S.158)

holds for some
t=3,...,T
. In the Base case, we have shown that this is a valid assumption for
t=3
.
Then, according to eq. (
47
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{s}_{t}&=\hat{s}_{t-1}+\lambda(\tilde{r}_{t-1}-\hat{s}_{t-1})\\
&=(1-\lambda)\hat{s}_{t-1}+\lambda\tilde{r}_{t-1}\\
&=(1-\lambda)\left((1-\lambda)^{t-2}\hat{s}_{1}+\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{t-2}(1-\lambda)^{t-2-j}\tilde{r}_{j}\right)+\lambda\tilde{r}_{t-1}\\
&=(1-\lambda)(1-\lambda)^{t-2}\hat{s}_{1}+(1-\lambda)\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{t-2}(1-\lambda)^{t-2-j}\tilde{r}_{j}+\lambda\tilde{r}_{t-1}\\
&=(1-\lambda)^{t-1}\hat{s}_{1}+\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{t-2}(1-\lambda)(1-\lambda)^{t-2-j}\tilde{r}_{j}+(1-\lambda)^{0}\lambda\tilde{r}_{t-1}\\
&=(1-\lambda)^{t-1}\hat{s}_{1}+\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{t-2}(1-\lambda)^{t-1-j}\tilde{r}_{j}+(1-\lambda)^{t-1-(t-1)}\lambda\tilde{r}_{t-1}\\
&=(1-\lambda)^{t-1}\hat{s}_{1}+\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{t-1}(1-\lambda)^{t-1-j}\tilde{r}_{j}.\\
\end{split}

(S.159)

∎

Theorem S.11
. The figure shows the histogram-based estimation of
p(d_{t}=1|\hat{s}_{t}=s)
based on simulated joint observations of

and
\hat{s}_{t}
as generated during model validation (
\hat{p}(d_{t}=1|\hat{s}_{t}=s)
, gray bars) and the analytical result of
Theorem S.11
(blue lines). For varying levels of
\lambda
, a good match is observed. Note that for
\lambda
values approaching
1
, the observed probability state estimates oscillate between values close to the
\tilde{r}_{t-1}
values of
0
or
1
, generating no observations of
d_{1}=1
and
\hat{s}_{t}\lesssim 0.9
. For full analytical details, please refer to
abm_figure_S9.py

.

Theorem S.12

(Conditional log-likelihood function)
.

Let

p(s_{1},d_{1:T},a_{1:T},r_{1:T})\\
=p(s_{1})p(d_{1})p(a_{1}|d_{1})p(r_{1}|s_{1},a_{1})\prod_{t=2}^{T}p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(a_{t}|d_{t})p(r_{t}|s_{1},a_{t})

(S.160)

denote the ABM induced by eqs. eq. (
24
), eq. (
51
), and
eq.~\eqref{eq:D-2}
. Then

p(a_{1:T}|r_{1:T})=\prod_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{d_{t}}p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(a_{t}|d_{t})

(S.161)

and

\sum_{d_{t}}p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(a_{t}|d_{t})=\left\llbracket\hat{s}_{t}(\lambda)\leq 1-\hat{s}_{t}(\lambda)\right\rrbracket\mbox{B}(a_{t};\tau)+\left\llbracket\hat{s}_{t}(\lambda)>1-\hat{s}_{t}(\lambda)\right\rrbracket\mbox{B}(a_{t};1-\tau).

(S.162)

Proof.

Given the identical forms of the ABMs in
Theorem S.9
and the current theorem, eq. (
S.161
) follows as in the proof of

Theorem S.9
. With respect to eq. (
S.162
), we have with
Theorem S.11
and eq. (
41
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{d_{t}}&p(d_{t}|a_{1:t-1},r_{1:t-1})p(a_{t}|d_{t})\\
&=\sum_{d_{t}}\left\llbracket\hat{s}_{t}(\lambda)\leq 1-\hat{s}_{t}(\lambda)\right\rrbracket^{1-d_{t}}\left\llbracket\hat{s}_{t}(\lambda)>1-\hat{s}_{t}(\lambda)\right\rrbracket^{d_{t}}\mbox{B}(a_{t};1-\tau)^{d_{t}}\mbox{B}(a_{t};\tau)^{1-d_{t}}\\
&=\left\llbracket\hat{s}_{t}(\lambda)\leq 1-\hat{s}_{t}(\lambda)\right\rrbracket\mbox{B}(a_{t};\tau)+\left\llbracket\hat{s}_{t}(\lambda)>1-\hat{s}_{t}(\lambda)\right\rrbracket\mbox{B}(a_{t};1-\tau)\end{split}

(S.163)

∎
