Figure S.22:
(Figure on previous page.)
Exemplary conditional log-likelihood functions of the form of eq. (
54
) and based on
Theorem S.12
. These conditional log-likelihood functions are based on the joint observations of actions and rewards for a single simulated participant dataset (10 blocks with 30 trials each) as these functions form the basis for parameter estimation and marginal likelihood approximation
Section 3.3
. The true, but unknown, parameter post-decision noise parameter value for each simulated dataset are given above each subpanel, the true, but unknown, parameter combination is indicated by white crosses on the respective log-likelihood surfaces. The color coding of the log-likelihood values is identical across all plots. Panel A depicts a low post-decision noise scenario with
\tau=0.1250
. Here, the conditional log-likelihood functions exhibit adequate curvature for the numerical maximization for a low true, but unknown, learning rate parameter of
\lambda=0.1
and a high true, but unknown, learning rate parameter of
\lambda=0.9
. However, varying the true, but unknown, learning rate parameter from
\lambda=0.5
to
\lambda=0.9
has hardly any effect on the resulting conditional log-likelihood function. This means that the observable data under this model are non-informative with respect to learning rates between 0.5 and 1.0 as these do not alter the selected actions in a systematic manner. This is mirrored in the observed non-identifiability of the learning rate parameter in this domain subset (cf.
Figure 8
D, left subpanel). For the higher post-decision noise scenario in Panel B (
\tau=0.35
), the conditional log-likelihood function curvature is reduced. Finally, for the high post-decision noise scenario in Panel C (
\tau=0.5
), curvature with respect to
\lambda
disappears, essentially removing indicative information about the learning rate parameter from the conditional profile log-likelihood function and hampering the successful numerical identification of the true, but unknown, learning rate parameter as observed in the constant parameter recovery results for this post-decision noise level (cf.
Figure 8
D, left subpanel). For full analytical details, please refer to
abm_figure_S11.py
.

S10  Protected exceedance probabilities

S10.1
Group-level model inference

The Bayesian model selection approach for group studies from which protected exceedance probabilities (PEPs) are derived was originally proposed by
130
and refined by
110
. We refer to this framework as
group-level model inference
(GMI), and in what follows, we present a unified documentation of the framework along with its derivation. To this end, we proceed as follows: first, we formulate the GMI probabilistic model; then, we discuss how PEPs are defined under the GMI framework; and finally, we address how PEPs are estimated based on a group of behavioral datasets using a free-form variational inference approach. We briefly review the theoretical background of this free-form variational inference approach in
Section S10.2
. Crucially, for group-level model inference, the GMI framework only requires log model evidences from individual participant datasets. We discuss our chosen approach to estimating log model evidences in terms of the Bayesian information criterion (BIC) in
Section S10.3
. Throughout, we defer proofs to
Section S10.4
.

In terms of notation, we denote probabilities by
\mathbb{P}
and probability mass/density functions (PMFs/PDFs) by standard lowercase letters such as
p
or
q
. We denote the PDF of the Dirichlet distribution of a
m
-dimensional random vector
\pi
taking on values in the
m-1
-dimensional probability simplex and with parameter vector
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{>0}
by
\mbox{Dir}(\pi;\alpha)
, and we denote the PMF of a random
m
-dimensional vector
\mu
taking on values in the set of
m
-dimensional canonical unit vectors
E_{m}:=\{e_{1},...,e_{m}\}
and with parameter vector
\pi
by
\mbox{Mult}(\mu;\pi)
(cf.
Table S.3
).

Model formulation

To formalize the GMI model, let
i=1,...,n
index participants and let

j=1,...,m
index behavioral models of the model space, such that

n
denotes the total number of participants and
m
denotes the total
number of models in the model space. The GMI model is composed of the following
random entities:

•

A
hypothesis

\eta
taking on values in
\{0,1\}
, for which
\eta=0

encodes the
null hypothesis
of all equal model probabilities, and

\eta=1
encodes the
alternative hypothesis
of not all equal model probabilities.
From an epistemic viewpoint, it is assumed that there exists a true but unknown hypothesis
value in the world, and the probabilities assigned to
\eta
encode
our uncertainty about this value.

•

A vector of
model probabilities

\pi
taking on values in
[0,1]^{m}
with
\sum_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{j}=1
.

\pi=(\pi_{1},...,\pi_{m})^{T}
is thus an
m
-dimensional vector with components

\pi_{j}\in[0,1]
for
j=1,...,n
summing to one, such as
\pi=(0.2,0.1,0.3,0.4)^{T}

for
m=4
. The
j
th entry
\pi_{j}
encodes the probability of model
j
. Again, from an epistemic
viewpoint, it is assumed that there exist true but unknown model probability
values in the world, and the probabilities assigned to
\pi
encode our
uncertainty about these values. As above, the probabilities assigned to the
\pi_{i}
s
encode our uncertainty about their values.

•

A set of
unobserved participant model indicators

\mu_{i}
taking on values in
\{0,1\}^{m}
with
\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{i_{j}}=1
for
i=1,...,n
.
\mu_{i}
is thus an
m
-dimensional canonical unit vector (sometimes
referred to as a
one-hot vector
or a
1-of-K vector
) comprising all
0
s except
for a single
1
, the index of which indicates the behavioral model index that
generates the behavioral model of participant
i
. We
denote the set of
m
-dimensional canonical unit vectors, i.e., the outcome set
of the
\mu_{i}
’s by
E_{m}:=\{e_{1},...,e_{m}\}
. For example, for
m=4
,

\mu_{16}=e_{3}=(0,0,1,0)^{T}
encodes that the behavioral data of participant

i=16
is generated by model
j=3
. We denote the set of all unobserved participant
model indicators by
\mu_{1:n}:=\{\mu_{1},...,\mu_{n}\}
.

•

The
observed participant behavioral datasets

y_{i}
for
i=1,...,n
. No particular
assumptions are made about the form of the
y_{i}
and their outcome sets. As an example,
y_{i}
could
be the vector of all actions taken by a participant in a sequential learning task or a neuroimaging data time-series. The probabilities assigned to the
y_{i}
encode our uncertainty about the model-based
data generative process, which is sometimes referred to as
observation error
.
We denote the set of all observed participant behavioral datasets by
y_{1:n}:=\{y_{1},...,y_{n}\}
.

The GMI model is given by the joint distribution of
\eta,\pi,\mu_{1:n}
and
y_{1:n}
,
which is assumed to factorize as follows:

p(\eta,\pi,\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n})=p(\eta)p(\pi|\eta)\prod_{i=1}^{n}p(\mu_{i}|\pi)p(y_{i}|\mu_{i}).

(S.164)

We unpack the components of eq. (
S.164
) below

•

The marginal PMF of the hypothesis is given by

p(\eta):=\frac{1}{2}\mbox{ for }\eta=0\mbox{ and }\eta=1.

(S.165)

From a Bayesian perspective, eq. (
S.165
) encodes our prior probability that
the null hypothesis of all equal model probabilities holds. This probability
(and its complementary probability, the probability that the alternative hypothesis
of not all equal model probabilities holds) is assumed to take on the value

1/2
, encoding maximal prior uncertainty regarding the hypothesis state.

•

The conditional PMF/PDF of the model probabilities is given by

p(\pi|\eta=0):=\begin{cases}1&\mbox{ if }\pi_{j}=\frac{1}{m}\mbox{ for all }j\in\mathbb{N}_{m}\\
0&\mbox{ if }\pi_{j}\neq\frac{1}{m}\mbox{ for all }j\in\mathbb{N}_{m}\end{cases},

(S.166)

and

p(\pi|\eta=1):=\mbox{Dir}(\pi;\alpha):=\frac{\Gamma\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}\right)}{\prod_{j=1}^{m}\Gamma(\alpha_{j})}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{j}^{\alpha_{j}-1}\mbox{ for }\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{>0}.

(S.167)

For
\eta=0
, the conditional PMF is of Dirac delta form and assigns
a probability mass of 1 to all equal model probabilities
\pi_{j}:=1/m
for
j=1,...,m
. Note that in this scenario
p(\pi|\eta=0)
is infinitely precise and encodes
the absence of uncertainty about the model probabilities
\pi
. For
\eta=1
,
the conditional PDF is of Dirichlet form and encodes our uncertainty about the
behavioral model probabilities
\pi
as a function of the prior parameter vector
\alpha
. For the common choice of
\alpha:=1_{m}
, this uncertainty is maximal in the
sense of a continuous uniform distribution on the
m-1
dimensional probability simplex.

•

The conditional PMF of the unobserved participant model indicators is given by the Multinoulli distribution

p(\mu_{i}|\pi):=\mbox{Mult}(\mu_{i};\pi):=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{j}^{\mu_{i_{j}}}.

(S.168)

p(\mu_{i}|\pi)
thus assigns the probability
\pi_{j}
to the
j
th canonical unit vector.
For example, for
m=4
and
\mu_{i}=(0,1,0,0)^{T}
, we have

p\left((0,1,0,0)^{T}|\pi\right)=\pi_{1}^{0}\cdot\pi_{2}^{1}\cdot\pi_{3}^{0}\cdot\pi_{4}^{0}=1\cdot\pi_{2}\cdot 1\cdot 1=\pi_{2}.

(S.169)

•

Like the precise form of the
y_{i},i=1,...,n
, the conditional PMF/PDF of
the observed participant data
y_{i}
is left unspecified but is assumed to exist.

The factorization properties of the joint distribution of
\eta,\pi.\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n}

given by eq. (
S.164
) encode two important conditional independence properties. First,
given the model probabilities
\pi
, the unobserved model indicators
\mu_{1},...,\mu_{n}

are identically and independently distributed. The model indicator value of the

i
th participant is thus assumed to have no influence on the model indicator of
the
j
th participant. Likewise, given the model indicator
\mu_{i}
, the observed
behavioral datasets
y_{1},...,y_{n}
are identically and independently distributed,
again encoding the absence of influences between participants.

Protected exceedance probabilities

The core quantity of interest in the GMI framework is the conditional probability
of the
j
th model probability
\pi_{j}
being the largest among all
m
model probabilities given the observed behavioral data
y_{1},...,y_{n}
. This conditional
probability has been dubbed the
protected exceedance probability of model
j
and is written here as

\varphi_{j}:=\mathbb{P}\left(\pi_{j}=\max\{\pi_{1},...,\pi_{m}\}|y_{1:n}\right).

(S.170)

Note that, naturally,

\sum_{j=1}^{m}\varphi_{j}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{P}\left(\pi_{j}=\max\{\pi_{1},...,\pi_{m}\}|y_{1:n}\right)=1,

(S.171)

i.e., protected exceedance probabilities sum to 1 over all models in the model space.
In
Section S10.4
, we show that the
j
th protected exceedance probability can be written as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varphi_{j}&=\mathbb{P}(\pi_{j}=\max\{\pi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}|\eta=0,y_{1:n})\mathbb{P}(\eta=0|y_{1:n})\\
&+\mathbb{P}(\pi_{j}=\max\{\pi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}|\eta=1,y_{1:n})(1-\mathbb{P}(\eta=0|y_{1:n})).\end{split}

(S.172)

The protected exceedance probability of model
j
can thus be expressed in terms of first, the exceedance probability of model
j
conditional on the null hypothesis and the observed data, second, the exceedance probability of model
j
conditional on the alternative hypothesis and the observed data, and third, the probability of the null hypothesis given the observed data. As shown in
Section S10.4
, the latter quantity can be rewritten as

\mathbb{P}(\eta=0|y_{1:n})=\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(\ln\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=1)-\ln\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=0)\right)}.

(S.173)

Together, eq. (
S.172
) and eq. (
S.173
) set the stage for inference in the GMI model. First, to evaluate the hypothesis- and observed data-conditional probabilities that
\pi_{j}
is the largest among all model probabilities (eq. (
S.172
)), it is necessary to evaluate the conditional PMFs/PDFs
p(\pi|\eta=0,y_{1:n})
and
p(\pi|\eta=1,y_{1:n})
of
\pi
given the hypothesis
\eta
and the observed data
y_{1:n}
. Second, to evaluate the probability of the null hypothesis given the observed data, it is further necessary to evaluate the log marginal likelihoods of the observed data given the hypothesis
\ln p(y_{1:n}|\eta=0)

and
\ln p(y_{1:n}|\eta=1)
(eq. (
S.173
)). Together, these two
tasks correspond to the fundamental aims of probabilistic inference as reviewed in
Section S10.2
.

Inference

To infer the necessary quantities for the evaluation of the
j
th model’s
protected exceedance probability given by the right-hand side of eq. (
S.172
),

110
, we use a two-step inference approach. In the first step, the required
conditional distribution of
\pi
, the resulting exceedance probability of model

j
, as well as the log marginal likelihood of the observed data
y
, are
evaluated
conditional on the null hypothesis
\eta=0
. In the second step,
the equivalent quantities are then evaluated
conditional on the alternative
hypothesis
\eta=1
. Each step employs a free-form mean-field variational
inference approach to the respective hypothesis-conditional probabilistic model.
To simplify the documentation of these steps, we define the
null hypothesis-conditional GMI model

p_{0}(\pi,\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n}):=p(\pi,\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n}|\eta=0)=p_{0}(\pi)\prod_{i=1}^{n}p(\mu_{i}|\pi)p(y_{i}|\mu_{i}),

(S.174)

where

p_{0}(\pi):=p(\pi|\eta=0),

(S.175)

and the

alternative hypothesis-conditional GMI model

p_{1}(\pi,\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n}):=p(\pi,\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n}|\eta=1)=p_{1}(\pi)\prod_{i=1}^{n}p(\mu_{i}|\pi)p(y_{i}|\mu_{i}),

(S.176)

where

p_{1}(\pi):=p(\pi|\eta=1).

(S.177)

The introduction of the null hypothesis-conditional GMI model is the core contribution of
110
, while the inference approach for the alternative hypothesis-conditional GMI was already established by
130
. In the following two subsections, we discuss the inference approaches for eq. (
S.174
) and eq. (
S.176
) in turn.

Inference conditional on
\eta=0

110
proposed a free-form variational inference algorithm for the null
hypothesis-conditional GMI model

p_{0}(\pi,\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n})=p_{0}(\pi)\prod_{i=1}^{n}p(\mu_{i}|\pi)p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})

(S.178)

based on the mean-field approximation

q_{0}(\pi,\mu_{1:n}):=q_{0}(\pi)\prod_{i=1}^{n}q_{0}(\mu_{i})

(S.179)

such that, upon the convergence of the algorithm,

p_{0}(\pi|y_{1:n})\approx q_{0}(\pi),\hskip 9.24994ptp_{0}(\mu_{1:n}|y_{1:n})\approx\prod_{i=1}^{n}q_{0}(\mu_{i})

(S.180)

and

\ln p_{0}(y_{1:n})\approx\mbox{ELBO}\left(q_{0}(\pi,\mu_{1:n})\right).

(S.181)

Two things are noteworthy here.

•

110
state that

p_{0}(\pi|y_{1:n})=p(\pi|\eta=0)

(S.182)

and evaluate the entailing model exceedance probability as

\mathbb{P}(\pi_{j}=\max\{\pi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}|\eta=0,y_{1:n})=\frac{1}{m}.

(S.183)

While this is very intuitive, we prove it in
Section S10.4
.

•

As no variational distribution on
\pi
is required, an iterative variational
inference algorithm, which iterates between updates of the variational distributions on

\mu_{1:n}
and
\pi
, is not necessary. In fact,
110
determines the variational
distributions
q(\mu_{i})
in a single step. Similarly, the ELBO is a function of the
q(\mu_{i})
only.

Variational distribution updates for
q(\mu_{i})

As shown in
Section S10.4
, for algorithm iteration
k=0
, the variational
distribution updates for
\mu_{i}
with
i=1,...,n
take the form

q^{(k+1)}_{0}(\mu_{i})=\mbox{Mult}\left(\mu_{i};\pi^{(k+1)}_{i}\right),

(S.184)

where

\pi_{i}^{(k+1)}:=\left(\pi_{ij}^{(k+1)}\right)_{j=1,...,m}\mbox{ with }\pi_{ij}^{(k+1)}:=\frac{\exp\left(\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i}=e_{j})\right)}{\sum_{\mu_{i}\in E_{m}}\exp\left(\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\right)}.

(S.185)

The
(k+1)
th variational distribution of
\mu_{i}
is thus given by a Multinoulli
distribution with a parameter vector depending on the participant-specific log
model evidence conditional on the
j
th model,
\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i}=e_{j})
. Notably, it is this
latter quantity that provides the link between participant- and model-specific maximum-likelihood
estimation and the evaluation of PEPs at the group-level because participant-specific
log model evidences can be approximated using BIC scores (cf.
Section S10.3
).

Log marginal likelihood

Further, as shown in
Section S10.4
, the ELBO approximation of the log marginal likelihood
in the current scenario evaluates to

\mbox{ELBO}\left(q_{0}^{(c)}(\mu_{1:n})\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{(c)}\left(\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i}=e_{j})-\ln m-\ln\pi_{ij}^{(c)}\right)

(S.186)

where
c:=k=1
. We thus have

\ln p_{0}(y_{1:n})\approx\mbox{ELBO}\left(q_{0}^{(c)}(\mu_{1:n})\right).

(S.187)

Inference conditional on
\eta=1

130
proposed a free-form variational inference algorithm for the alternative
hypothesis-conditional GMI

p_{1}(\pi,\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n})=p_{1}(\pi)\prod_{i=1}^{n}p(\mu_{i}|\pi)p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})

(S.188)

based on the mean-field approximation

q_{1}(\pi,\mu_{1:n}):=q_{1}(\pi)\prod_{i=1}^{n}q_{1}(\mu_{i})

(S.189)

such that, upon the convergence of the algorithm,

p_{1}(\pi|y_{1:n})\approx q_{1}(\pi),\hskip 9.24994ptp_{1}(\mu_{1:n}|y_{1:n})\approx\prod_{i=1}^{n}q_{1}(\mu_{i}),

(S.190)

and

\ln p_{1}(y_{1:n})\approx\mbox{ELBO}\left(q_{1}(\pi,\mu_{1:n})\right).

(S.191)

In the following, we first state the entailing variational distribution update
equations that constitute a coordinate-wise ascent variational inference (CAVI)
algorithm (cf.
Section S10.2
. We then consider
the Monte Carlo approach employed to estimate the model exceedance probabilities
given the alternative hypothesis and finally document the approximation of
the log marginal likelihood of the observed data given the alternative hypothesis
furnished by the algorithm’s ELBO.

Variational distribution updates for
q(\mu_{i})

As shown in
Section S10.4
, for algorithm iterations
k=0,1,2,...
, the
variational distribution updates for
\mu_{i}
with
i=1,...,n
take the form

q^{(k+1)}_{1}(\mu_{i})=\mbox{Mult}\left(\mu_{i};\pi^{(k+1)}_{i}\right),\mbox{where }\pi_{i}^{(k+1)}:=\left(\pi_{ij}^{(k+1)}\right)_{j=1,...,m}

(S.192)

with

\pi_{ij}^{(k+1)}:=\frac{\exp\left(\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i}=e_{j})+\psi\left(\alpha_{j}^{(k)}\right)-\psi\left(\alpha_{s}^{(k)}\right)\right)}{\sum_{\mu_{i}\in E_{m}}\exp\left(\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})+\psi\left(\alpha_{j}^{(k)}\right)-\psi\left(\alpha_{s}^{(k)}\right)\right)},

(S.193)

where
\psi
denotes the digamma function and
\alpha_{s}^{(k)}:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}^{(k)}
.
The
(k+1)
th variational distribution of
\mu_{i}
is thus given by a Multinoulli
distribution with a parameter vector depending on the participant-specific log
model evidence conditional on the
j
th model,
\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i}=e_{j})
(again providing
the link between participant- and model-specific maximum-likelihood
estimation and the evaluation of PEPs at the group-level), and
the parameters of the variational distribution of
\pi
on the
k
th iteration of the
algorithm.

Variational distribution updates for
q(\pi)

As shown in
Section S10.4
, for algorithm iterations
k=0,1,2,...
, the variational
distribution updates for
\pi
take the form

q^{(k+1)}_{1}(\pi):=\mbox{Dir}\left(\pi,\alpha^{(k+1)}\right)\mbox{, where }\alpha^{(k+1)}_{j}:=\alpha_{j}+\beta_{j}^{(k)}

(S.194)

with

\beta_{j}^{(k)}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{ij}^{(k)}\mbox{ for }i=1,...,n,j=1,...,m.

(S.195)

The
(k+1)
th variational distribution of
\pi
is thus a Dirichlet distribution
with a parameter vector depending on the prior distribution parameters

\alpha_{j}
(cf. eq. (
S.167
)) and the parameters of the variational distribution of

\mu
on the
k
th iteration of the algorithm
\pi_{ij}^{(k)}
(cf. eq. (
S.192
)).

The variational updates of eq. (
S.192
) and eq. (
S.194
) are iterated alternately
until the algorithm converges. We denote the iteration of convergence by
c
,
such that upon the convergence of the algorithm, we have

p_{1}(\pi|y_{1:n})\approx q^{(c)}_{1}(\pi)=\mbox{Dir}\left(\pi,\alpha^{(c)}\right)

(S.196)

and

p_{1}(\mu_{1:n}|y_{1:n})\approx\prod_{i=1}^{n}q_{1}^{(c)}(\mu_{i})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mbox{Mult}\left(\mu_{i};\pi^{(c)}_{i}\right).

(S.197)

Exceedance probability

To evaluate the exceedance probability of model
j
conditional on
\eta=1
,

130
and
110
use a Monte Carlo integration approach. To illustrate
this, let

\pi^{(1)},...,\pi^{(s)}\sim\mbox{Dir}\left(\pi;\alpha^{(c)}\right)

(S.198)

denote a set of
s
independent samples from the converged variational distribution
on
\pi
, and for
j=1,...,m
and
k=1,...,s
, let

\mathbb{I}_{j}:[0,1]^{m}\to\{0,1\},\pi^{(k)}\mapsto\mathbb{I}_{j}\left(\pi^{(k)}\right):=\begin{cases}1&\mbox{ if }\pi_{j}^{(k)}=\max\{\pi_{1}^{(k)},...,\pi_{m}^{(k)}\}\\
0&\mbox{ if }\pi_{j}^{(k)}\neq\max\{\pi_{1}^{(k)},...,\pi_{m}^{(k)}\}\\
\end{cases}

(S.199)

denote the indicator function of the event that
\pi_{j}
is the largest of
the model probabilities
\pi_{1},...,\pi_{m}
. Then
130
and
110
use

\hat{\mathbb{P}}(\pi_{j}=\max\{\pi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}|\eta=1,y_{1:n}):=\frac{1}{s}\sum_{k=1}^{s}\mathbb{I}_{j}\left(\pi^{(k)}\right)

(S.200)

as an estimate of the exceedance probability of the
j
th model conditional on
\eta=1
.
In our implementation of the approach, we set
s:=10^{6}
and use the ‘scipy.stats‘
module to sample from the Dirichlet distribution with parameter
\alpha^{(c)}
.

Log marginal likelihood

Finally, as shown in
Section S10.4
, the ELBO approximation of the log marginal likelihood
in the current scenario evaluates to

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mbox{ELBO}\left(q_{1}^{(c)}(\pi,\mu_{1:n})\right)=\\
&\ln\Gamma\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}\right)-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\ln\Gamma(\alpha_{j})+\sum_{j=1}^{m}(\alpha_{j}-1)\left(\psi\left(\alpha_{j}^{(c)}\right)-\psi\left(\alpha_{s}^{(c)}\right)\right)\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{(c)}\left(\psi\left(\alpha_{j}^{(c)}\right)-\psi\left(\alpha_{s}^{(c)}\right)\right)\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{(c)}\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i}=e_{j})\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt-\ln\Gamma\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}^{(c)}\right)+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\ln\Gamma\left(\alpha_{j}^{(c)}\right)-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\alpha_{j}^{(c)}-1\right)\left(\psi\left(\alpha_{j}^{(c)}\right)-\psi\left(\alpha_{s}^{(c)}\right)\right)\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{(c)}\ln\pi_{ij}^{(c)},\end{split}

(S.201)

where
\Gamma
denotes the Gamma function. Note that the
\alpha_{j}
’s refer
to the parameters of the prior distribution of
\pi
(cf. eq. (
S.167
), while the

\alpha_{j}^{(c)}
s refer to the parameters of the converged variational distribution
of
\pi
. Upon convergence of the algorithm, we thus have

\ln p_{1}(y_{1:n})\approx\mbox{ELBO}\left(q_{1}^{(c)}(\pi,\mu_{1:n})\right).

(S.202)

Summary

In summary, the protected exceedance probability
\varphi_{j}
of model
j
can be
evaluated based on eq. (
S.170
) and eq. (
S.172
) by approximating
the participant-specific log model evidence conditional on the
j
th model using the corresponding BIC score

\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i}=e_{j}):=\mbox{BIC}_{ij},

(S.203)

and setting

\mathbb{P}(\pi_{j}=\max\{\pi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}|\eta=0,y_{1:n}):=\frac{1}{m},

(S.204)

\mathbb{P}(\pi_{j}=\max\{\pi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}|\eta=1,y_{1:n}):=\hat{\mathbb{P}}(\pi_{j}=\max\{\pi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}|\eta=1,y_{1:n}),

(S.205)

\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=0):=\mbox{ELBO}\left(q_{1}^{(c)}(\mu_{1:n})\right)

(S.206)

and

\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=1):=\mbox{ELBO}\left(q_{1}^{(c)}(\pi,\mu_{1:n})\right).

(S.207)

S10.2
Free-form mean-field variational inference

In this Appendix, we review the theoretical background of free-form mean-field
variational inference in condensed form, with proofs deferred to
Section S10.4
. For
extended introductions to variational inference, see, for example,
42
,

97
, and
14
. We commence by introducing the fundamental problem of probabilistic inference.

Definition 1

(Fundamental problem of probabilistic inference)
.

Let
y:=(y_{1},...,y_{n})
denote a set of observable random
entities modeling observed data, and let
z:=(z_{1},...,z_{m})
denote a set of latent random
entities. Assume that
y
and
z
form a probabilistic model with PDF/PMF

p(y,z)=p(y|z)p(z).

(S.208)

Then, the fundamental problem of probabilistic inference is to approximate (1) the conditional distribution
p(z|y)
, and
(2) the log model evidence
\ln p(y)=\ln\int p(y,z)\,dz
.

In Bayesian parameter estimation problems, the conditional distribution
p(z|y)

is often referred to as the
posterior distribution
, and the log model evidence
is also referred to as the
log marginal likelihood
. Note that for any fixed
dataset, the log model evidence is a fixed scalar quantity. In our current application
to the hypothesis-conditional GMIs, the set of observable random entities
y
is given by
the set of observed participant behavioral datasets
y_{1},...,y_{n}
, while the set of
latent random entities
z
is given by the union of the model probabilities
\pi

and the unobserved participant model indicators
\mu_{1},...,\mu_{n}
. The following
theorem is central to the variational inference approach.

Theorem S.13

(Log model evidence decomposition)
.

Let
p(y,z)
denote a probabilistic model, and let
q(z)
denote the PDF/PMF of a
distribution over the latent random variables, referred to as the variational distribution.
Then the following log model evidence decomposition holds

\ln p(y)=\mbox{ELBO}(q(z))+\mbox{KL}(q(z)||p(z|y)),

(S.209)

where

\mbox{ELBO}(q(z)):=\int q(z)\ln\left(\frac{p(y,z)}{q(z)}\right)\,dz

(S.210)

is referred to as the evidence lower bound, and

\mbox{KL}(q(z)||p(z|y)):=\int q(z)\ln\left(\frac{q(z)}{p(z|y)}\right)\,dz

(S.211)

is referred to as the Kullback-Leibler divergence between
q(z)
and
p(z|y)
.

The variational distribution
q(z)
serves as an approximation to the conditional
distribution
p(z|y)
, and, as alluded to below, the ELBO serves as an approximation to
the log model evidence. Commonly,
q(z)
is of parameterized functional form, and
we refer to its parameters as
variational parameters
. Owing to its origin in
statistical physics, the ELBO is often referred to as
variational free energy
.
The Kullback-Leibler divergence serves as a distance measure between the variational
distribution
q(z)
and the conditional distribution
p(z|y)
. The following
properties of the Kullback-Leibler divergence enable variational inference. The
first of these is a direct consequence of Jensen’s inequality
(
67
)
.

Theorem S.14

(Kullback-Leibler divergence)
.

Let
\mbox{KL}\left(q(z)||p(z)\right)
denote the Kullback-Leibler divergence
of two PMFs/PDFs
q(z)
and
p(z)
. Then,

\mbox{KL}\left(q(z)||p(z)\right)>0\mbox{ for }q(z)\neq p(z)

(S.212)

and

\mbox{KL}\left(q(z)||p(z)\right)=0\mbox{ for }q(z)=p(z).

(S.213)

The non-negativity of the Kullback-Leibler divergence endows the ELBO with its
characterizing property:

Theorem S.15

(Evidence lower bound)
.

The ELBO is a lower bound of the log model evidence,

\mbox{ELBO}(q(z))\leq\ln p(y).

(S.214)

The fixed scalar nature of
\ln p(y)
for a given dataset and the fact that
the ELBO is always smaller than or equal to
\ln p(y)
suggest two equivalent options
for solving the fundamental problem of probabilistic inference using a variational
approach: either (1) minimizing
\mbox{KL}(q(z)||p(z|y))
with respect to
q(z)
,
such that
q(z)\to p(z|y)
and
\mbox{ELBO}(q(z))\to\ln p(y)
, or maximizing

\mbox{ELBO}(q(z))
with respect to
q(z)
, such that likewise

\mbox{ELBO}(q(z))\to\ln p(y)
and
q(z)\to p(z|y)
. The free-form
variational inference approach discussed below follows the latter approach. Its
final building block is the notion of a
mean-field variational approximation
,
which merely refers to an independence assumption on
q(z)

Definition 2

(Mean-field variational approximation and inference)
.

Let
z=(z_{1},...,z_{m})
denote the latent random variables of a probabilistic
model and let
\mathcal{S}:=(z_{s_{1}},...,z_{s_{S}})
denote a partition of
z

into
S
mutually exclusive subsets, such that
\cup_{i=1}^{S}z_{s_{i}}=z

and
z_{s_{i}}\cap z_{s_{j}}=\emptyset
for
i\neq j
. Then the assumption that

z_{s_{1}},...,z_{s_{S}}
form subsets of independent variables, i.e.,

q(z):=\prod_{i=1}^{S}q(z_{s_{i}}),

(S.215)

where
q(z_{s_{i}})
denotes the PDF/PMF of the distribution of the elements of

z_{s_{i}},i=1,...,S
is referred to as a mean-field approximation.

Clearly, variational mean-field approximations neglect potential non-independencies
in the conditional distribution
p(z|y)
. Commonly employed variational mean-field
approximations are a full factorization
q(z)=\prod_{i=1}^{m}q(z_{i})
or a
binary factorization
q(z)=q(z_{s})q(z_{\setminus s})
, where
Z_{s}\subset z
and

z_{\setminus s}:=z\setminus Z_{s}
. In our current application, a full factorization
is employed. We are now in a position to formulate the general free-form mean-field
variational inference approach that forms the foundation for inference in the
current application and gives rise to the variational distribution update equations
of the main text.

Theorem S.16

(Free-form mean-field variational inference)
.

Let
p(y,z)
denote a probabilistic model comprising observable random variables

\upsilon=(y_{1},...,y_{n})
and latent random variables
z=(z_{1},...,z_{m})
. Let
q(z)

denote a variational distribution and assume that
q(z)
factorizes in a binary
manner, i.e.,
q(z)=q(z_{s})q(z_{\setminus s})
with
Z_{s}\subset z

and
z_{\setminus s}:=z\setminus Z_{s}
. Then, setting

q(z_{s}):=\frac{1}{\gamma_{s}}\exp\left(\int q(z_{\setminus s})\ln p(y,z)\,dz_{\setminus s}\right),

(S.216)

where
\gamma_{s}
denotes a normalization constant independent of
z_{s}
, maximizes
the evidence lower bound with respect to
q(z_{s})
.

The functional form of the maximizing variational distributions depends on the
right-hand side of the proportionality statement; hence, ”free-form”. Note that
exchanging the roles of
q(z_{s})
and
q(z_{\setminus s})
maximizes the ELBO
with respect to
q(z_{\setminus s})
. This implies the following coordinate-wise
ELBO maximization approach, referred to as the coordinate-ascent variational inference
(CAVI) algorithm.

Definition 3

(Coordinate-ascent variational inference (CAVI))
.

Let
p(y,z)
denote a probabilistic model comprising observable random variables

y=(y_{1},...,y_{n})
and latent random variables
z=(z_{1},...,z_{m})
.
Let
q(z)
denote the PDF/PMF of a variational distribution and assume that
q(z)

factorizes in a binary manner. Then the following algorithm, referred to as
coordinate-ascent variational inference, maximizes the evidence lower bound:

Initialization

(0) Initialize
q^{(0)}(z_{s})
and
q^{(0)}(z_{\setminus s})
appropriately,
e.g., by setting
q^{(0)}(z_{s}):=\smallint p(z)\,dz_{\setminus s}
and

q^{(0)}(z_{\setminus s}):=\smallint p(z)\,dz_{s}
, evaluate

\mbox{ELBO}\left(q^{(0)}(z_{s})q^{(0)}(z_{\setminus s})\right)
, and select a
convergence criterion
\delta>0
.

Iterations

For
k=0,1,2,...

(1) Set

q^{(k+1)}(z_{s}):=\frac{1}{\gamma_{s}}\exp\left(\smallint q^{(k)}(z_{\setminus s})\ln p(y,z)\,dz_{\setminus s}\right)

(S.217)

(2) Set

q^{(k+1)}(z_{\setminus s}):=\frac{1}{\gamma_{\setminus s}}\exp\left(\smallint q^{(k+1)}(z_{s})\ln p(y,z)\,dz_{s}\right)

(S.218)

(3) Evaluate

\mbox{ELBO}\left(q^{(k+1)}(z_{s}),q^{(k+1)}(z_{\setminus s})\right)

(S.219)

until

\big|\mbox{ELBO}\left(q^{(k+1)}(z_{s})q^{(k+1)}(z_{\setminus s})\right)-\mbox{ELBO}\left(q^{(k)}(z_{s})q^{(k)}(z_{\setminus s})\right)\big|<\delta

(S.220)

Return

p(z|y)\approx q^{(k+1)}(z_{s})q^{(k+1)}(z_{\setminus s})\mbox{ and }\ln p(y)\approx\mbox{ELBO}\left(q^{(k+1)}(z_{s})q^{(k+1)}(z_{\setminus s})\right)

(S.221)

S10.3
Bayesian information criterion

Let

p(y,\theta)=p(y|\theta)p(\theta)

(S.222)

denote a probabilistic model, i.e., a joint distribution of data
y
and parameters
\theta
. The Bayesian information criterion (BIC) was proposed by
118
as an approximation of the log marginal likelihood

\ln p(y)=\ln\int p(y,\theta)\,d\theta.

(S.223)

The BIC is given by

\mbox{BIC}:=\ell(\hat{\theta})-\frac{k}{2}\ln n,

(S.224)

where
\ell(\hat{\theta}
denotes a maximum value of the log-likelihood
\ln p(y|\theta)
of the model,
k
denotes the dimension of
\theta
(i.e., the number of
scalar parameters of the probabilistic model), and
n
denotes the
number of data observations. The BIC formula rests on Laplace’s integral approximation method, which we first briefly review for the case of a multivariate real-valued function. To this end, let
f:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
denote a twice-differentiable convex multivariate real-valued function with a minimum at
\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Then
Laplace’s integral approximation method
corresponds to approximating the integral

I_{n}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}\exp(-nf(x))\,dx

(S.225)

for
n\in\mathbb{N}
as

I_{n}\approx\left(\frac{2\pi}{n}\right)^{\frac{k}{2}}|H^{f}(\tilde{x})|^{-\frac{1}{2}}\exp(-nf(\tilde{x})),

(S.226)

where
|H^{f}(\tilde{x})|
denotes the determinant of the Hessian of
f
evaluated at
\tilde{x}
. For the motivation and derivation of Laplace’s integral approximation method, see, e.g.,
81
. Using Laplace’s integral approximation method, it can readily be shown that an approximation of the log marginal likelihood
\ln p(y)
is furnished by

\ln p(y)\approx\frac{k}{2}\ln 2\pi-\frac{k}{2}\ln n-\frac{1}{2}\ln|H^{f}(\hat{\theta})|+\ln p(y|\hat{\theta})+\ln p(\hat{\theta}),

(S.227)

where
\hat{\theta}
denotes a maximum of the function

\ell:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R},\theta\mapsto\ell(\theta):=\ln p(y|\theta).

(S.228)

This may be seen as follows: Define

f:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R},\theta\mapsto f(\theta):=-\frac{1}{N}\left(\ln p(y|\theta)+\ln p(\theta)\right),

(S.229)

and let
\tilde{\theta}
denote a minimum location of
f
. Then, we have with the Laplace integral approximation of eq. (
S.226
)

\displaystyle\ln p(y)

\displaystyle=\ln\int p(y|\theta)p(\theta)\,d\theta

\displaystyle=\ln\int\exp\left(\ln\left(p(y|\theta)p(\theta)\right)\right)\,d\theta

\displaystyle=\ln\int\exp\left(\ln p(y|\theta)+\ln p(\theta)\right)\,d\theta

\displaystyle=\ln\int\exp\left(-n\left(-\frac{1}{n}\left(\ln p(y|\theta)+\ln p(\theta)\right)\right)\right)\,d\theta

\displaystyle=\ln\int\exp\left(-nf(\theta)\right)\,d\theta

\displaystyle\approx\ln\left(\left(\frac{2\pi}{n}\right)^{\frac{k}{2}}|H^{f}(\tilde{\theta})|^{-\frac{1}{2}}\exp(-nf(\tilde{\theta}))\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{k}{2}\ln 2\pi-\frac{k}{2}\ln n-\frac{1}{2}\ln|H^{f}(\tilde{\theta})|+\ln p(y|\tilde{\theta})+\ln p(\tilde{\theta}).

The BIC formula then results from (1) neglecting the constant terms
\frac{k}{2}\ln 2\pi
and
\ln p(\hat{\theta})
and (2) neglecting the term
-\frac{1}{2}\ln|H^{f}(\tilde{\theta})|
, which for
n\to\infty
is bounded from above. With
\ln p(y|\hat{\theta})=\ell(\hat{\theta})
it then follows that

\ln p(y)\approx\ell(\hat{\theta})-\frac{k}{2}\ln n=\mbox{BIC}.

(S.230)

S10.4
Proofs

Proof of eq. (
S.170
)

We have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}(\pi_{j}=\max\{\pi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}|y_{1:n})\\
&=\mathbb{P}(\pi_{j}=\max\{\pi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m},\eta=0|y_{1:n})+\mathbb{P}(\pi_{j}=\max\{\pi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m},\eta=1|y_{1:n})\\
&=\mathbb{P}(\pi_{j}=\max\{\pi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}|\eta=0,y_{1:n})\mathbb{P}(\eta=0|y_{1:n})\\
&+\mathbb{P}(\pi_{j}=\max\{\pi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}|\eta=1,y_{1:n})\mathbb{P}(\eta=1|y_{1:n})\\
&=\mathbb{P}(\pi_{j}=\max\{\pi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}|\eta=0,y_{1:n})\mathbb{P}(\eta=0|y_{1:n})\\
&+\mathbb{P}(\pi_{j}=\max\{\pi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}|\eta=1,y_{1:n})(1-\mathbb{P}(\eta=0|y_{1:n})).\\
\end{split}

(S.231)

\hfill\Box

Proof of eq. (
S.173
)

We have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{P}(\eta=0|y_{1:n})&=\frac{\mathbb{P}(\eta=0,y_{1:n})}{\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n})}\\
&=\frac{\mathbb{P}(\eta=0)\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=0)}{\sum_{\eta\in\{0,1\}}\mathbb{P}(\eta,y_{1:n})}\\
&=\frac{\mathbb{P}(\eta=0)\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=0)}{\sum_{\eta\in\{0,1\}}\mathbb{P}(\eta)\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta)}\\
&=\frac{\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=0)}{\sum_{\eta\in\{0,1\}}\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta)}\\
&=\frac{\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=0)}{\sum_{\eta\in\{0,1\}}\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta)}\\
&=\frac{\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=0)}{\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=0)+\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=1)}\\
&=\frac{\frac{\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=0)}{\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=0)}}{\frac{\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=0)+\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=1)}{\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=0)}}\\
&=\frac{1}{\frac{\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=0)}{\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=0)}+\frac{\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=1)}{\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=0)}}\\
&=\frac{1}{1+\frac{\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=1)}{\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=0)}}\\
&=\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(\ln\left(\frac{\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=1)}{\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=0)}\right)\right)}\\
&=\frac{1}{1+\exp\left(\ln\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=1)-\ln\mathbb{P}(y_{1:n}|\eta=0)\right)}.\end{split}

(S.232)

\hfill\Box

Proof of eq. (
S.185
)

We first note that the definition of
p(\pi|\eta=0)
in eq. (
S.166
) implies that

p(\pi=1_{m}m^{-1}|\eta=0)=1,

(S.233)

where
1_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
denotes a vector of all 1’s. We then note that, instead
of inference in the model specified by eq. (
S.185
), it suffices to delineate
the conditional distribution of
\mu_{1:n}
given
y_{1:n}
in the further reduced
model

p_{\tilde{0}}(\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n}):=p_{0}(\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n}|\eta=0,\pi=1_{m}m^{-1}),

(S.234)

because

\displaystyle\begin{split}p_{0}(\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n}|\eta=0,\pi=1_{m}m^{-1})&=\frac{p(\pi=1_{m}m^{-1},\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n}|\eta=0)}{p(\pi=1_{m}m^{-1}|\eta=0)}\\
&=\frac{p(\pi=1_{m}m^{-1},\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n}|\eta=0)}{1}\\
&=p(\pi=1_{m}m^{-1},\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n}|\eta=0).\\
\end{split}

(S.235)

For
\pi=1_{m}m^{-1}
, the GMI model conditional on
\eta=0
and the GMI
model conditional on
\eta=0

and

\pi=1_{m}m^{-1}
are identical, and the
respective distributions depend on
\mu_{1:n}
and
y_{1:n}
only. From
Section S10.2
and the definitions in eq. (
S.178
) and eq. (
S.179
) of the current inference scenario, we next see that the CAVI algorithm update for the variational distribution on
\mu_{i}

for
p_{\tilde{0}}(\mu_{1_{n}},y_{1:n})
takes the form

q(\mu_{i}):=\frac{1}{\gamma_{\mu_{i}}}\exp(I_{\mu_{i}})\mbox{ with }I_{\mu_{i}}:\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\ln p(\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n})\,d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}\mbox{ for }i=1,...,n,

(S.236)

where we omitted the iteration superscripts and the hypothesis subscripts for ease of notation.
To show the validity of eq. (
S.185
), we rewrite
I_{\mu_{i}}
in terms of the model indicator
conditional log model evidence
\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})
. We have

\displaystyle I_{\mu_{i}}

\displaystyle=\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\ln p(\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n})d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}

\displaystyle=\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\ln\left(\prod_{k=1}^{n}p(\mu_{k}|\pi)p(y_{k}|\mu_{k})\right)d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}

\displaystyle=\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\ln p(\mu_{k}|\pi)+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\ln p(y_{k}|\mu_{k})\right)d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}

\displaystyle=\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\sum_{k=1}^{n}\ln p(\mu_{k}|\pi)+\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\sum_{k=1}^{n}\ln p(y_{k}|\mu_{k})\,d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}

\displaystyle=\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\sum_{k=1}^{n}\ln p(\mu_{k}|\pi)d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}+\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\sum_{k=1}^{n}\ln p(y_{k}|\mu_{k})d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}d\pi

\displaystyle=\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\ln p(\mu_{k}|\pi)+\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}

\displaystyle+\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\ln p(y_{k}|\mu_{k})+\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}

\displaystyle=\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\ln p(\mu_{k}|\pi)d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}+\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}

\displaystyle+\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\ln p(y_{k}|\mu_{k})d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}+\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}

\displaystyle=\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}+\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}+c_{1}

\displaystyle=\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}+\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}+c_{1}

\displaystyle=\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)+\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})+c_{1}

\displaystyle=\ln\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\left(\frac{1}{m}\right)^{\mu_{ij}}\right)+\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})+c_{1}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{ij}\ln m+\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})+c_{1}

\displaystyle=\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})-\ln m+c_{1}

\displaystyle=\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})+c_{2},

where we defined the constant terms

\displaystyle\begin{split}c_{1}:=\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\ln p(\mu_{k}|\pi)d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}+\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\ln p(y_{k}|\mu_{k})d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}\end{split}

(S.237)

and

c_{2}:=c_{1}-\ln m,

(S.238)

which do not vary with
\mu_{i}
and the last equality follows from the
fact that for
\mu_{i}=e_{j},j=1,...,m
it holds that
\mu_{ij}=1
for

i=j

\mu_{ij}=0
for
i\neq j
. For the variational update of
q(\mu_{i})
, we thus obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}q(\mu_{i})&=\frac{1}{\gamma_{\mu_{i}}}\exp\left(I_{\mu_{i}}\right)\\
\Leftrightarrow q(\mu_{i})&=\frac{1}{\gamma_{\mu_{i}}}\exp\left(\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})+c_{2}\right)\\
\Leftrightarrow q(\mu_{i})&=\frac{1}{\gamma_{\mu_{i}}}\exp\left(\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\right)\exp(c_{2})\\
\Leftrightarrow q(\mu_{i})&=\frac{\exp(c_{2})\exp\left(\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\right)}{\sum_{\mu_{i}\in E_{m}}\exp(c_{2})\exp\left(\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\right)}\\
\Leftrightarrow q(\mu_{i})&=\frac{\exp\left(\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\right)}{\sum_{\mu_{i}\in E_{m}}\exp\left(\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\right)}.\end{split}

(S.239)

Finally, with
\mu_{i}\in E_{m}
, we may re-express the above in Multinoulli distribution
form by defining

\pi_{ij}^{q}:=\frac{\exp\left(\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i}=e_{j})\right)}{\sum_{\mu_{i}\in E_{m}}\exp\left(\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\right)},

(S.240)

such that

q(\mu_{i})=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\left(\pi_{ij}^{q}\right)^{\mu_{i_{j}}}.

(S.241)

In summary, we have, now re-introducing the algorithm iteration superscripts instead
of the variational superscript
q
as well as the hypothesis subscript,

q^{(k+1)}_{0}(\mu_{i})=\mbox{Mult}\left(\mu_{i};\pi_{i}^{(k+1)}\right),

(S.242)

where

\pi_{i}^{(k+1)}:=\left(\pi_{ij}^{(k+1)}\right)_{j=1,...,m}\mbox{ with }\pi_{ij}^{(k+1)}:=\frac{\exp\left(\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i}=e_{j})\right)}{\sum_{\mu_{i}\in E_{m}}\exp\left(\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\right)}.

(S.243)

\hfill\Box

Proof of eq. (
S.186
)

The ELBO in the current scenario is given by

\mbox{ELBO}(q(\mu_{1:n})):=\int q(\mu_{1:n})\ln\left(\frac{p(\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n})}{q(\mu_{1:n})}\right)\,d\mu_{1:n}.

(S.244)

In the following, we first rewrite the right-hand side of eq. (
S.244
) as a sum
of three integral terms. We then consider these integral terms and finally assemble the form
provided in eq. (
S.186
). Thus, we first note that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mbox{ELBO}(q(\mu_{1:n}))\\
&:=\int q(\mu_{1:n})\ln\left(\frac{p(\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n})}{q(\mu_{1:n})}\right)\,d\mu_{1:n}\\
&=\int q(\mu_{1:n})\left(\ln p(\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n})-\ln q(\mu_{1:n})\right)\,d\mu_{1:n}\\
&=\int q(\mu_{1:n})\ln p(\pi,\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n})\,d\mu_{1:n}-\int q(\mu_{1:n})\ln q(\mu_{1:n})\,d\mu_{1:n}\\
&=\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\ln\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}p(\mu_{i}|\pi)p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\right)\,d\mu_{1:n}-\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\ln\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\right)\,d\mu_{1:n}\\
&=\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\right)\,d\mu_{1:n}-\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln q(\mu_{i})\right)\,d\mu_{1:n}\\
&=\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)\,d\mu_{1:n}+\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\,d\mu_{1:n}\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt-\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln q(\mu_{i})\,d\mu_{1:n}.\end{split}

(S.245)

We consider the remaining integral terms in turn. For the first term, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}T_{1}&:=\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)\,d\mu_{1:n}\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int q(\mu_{i})\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)\,d\mu_{i}\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int q(\mu_{i})\ln\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m}\pi_{j}^{\mu_{ij}}\right)\,d\mu_{i}\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int q(\mu_{i})\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mu_{ij}\ln\pi_{j}\right)\,d\mu_{i}\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{\mu_{i}\in E_{m}}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mu_{ij}\ln\pi_{j}\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{q}\ln\pi_{j},\end{split}

(S.246)

where we defined

\pi_{ij}^{q}:=q(\mu_{i})\mbox{ for }\mu_{i}=e_{j}\mbox{ with }e_{j}\in E_{m}.

(S.247)

For the second term, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}T_{2}&:=\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\,d\mu_{1:n}\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int q(\mu_{i})\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\,d\mu_{i}\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{\mu_{i}\in E_{m}}q(\mu_{i})\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{q}\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i}=e_{j}).\end{split}

(S.248)

Finally, for the third term, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}T_{3}&=\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln q(\mu_{i})\,d\mu_{1:n}\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int q(\mu_{i})\ln q(\mu_{i})\,d\mu_{i}\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{\mu_{i}\in E_{m}}q(\mu_{i})\ln q(\mu_{i})\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{q}\ln\pi_{ij}^{q}.\end{split}

(S.249)

In summary, we thus obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mbox{ELBO}(q(\mu_{1:n}))&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{q}\ln\pi_{j}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{q}\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i}=e_{j})-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{q}\ln\pi_{ij}^{q}\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{q}\ln\left(\frac{1}{m}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{q}\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i}=e_{j})-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{q}\ln\pi_{ij}^{q}\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{q}\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i}=e_{j})-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{q}\ln m-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{q}\ln\pi_{ij}^{q}\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{q}\left(\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i}=e_{j})-\ln m-\ln\pi_{ij}^{q}\right).\end{split}

(S.250)

Replacing the variational superscripts on
p_{ij}
with the iteration counter
at convergence
(c)
yields the ELBO as provided in eq. (
S.186
).

\hfill\Box

Proof of eq. (
S.192
)

From
S10.2
and the definitions of
eq. (
S.188
) and eq. (
S.189
) in the current inference scenario, we see that the
CAVI algorithm update for the variational distribution on
\mu_{i}
takes the form

q(\mu_{i}):=\frac{1}{\gamma_{\mu_{i}}}\exp\left(I_{\mu_{i}}\right)\mbox{ with }I_{\mu_{i}}:=\int q(\pi)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\ln p(\pi,\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n})d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}d\pi,

(S.251)

where we omitted the iteration superscripts and the hypothesis subscripts for
ease of notation. To show the validity of eq. (
S.192
), we rewrite
I_{\mu_{i}}
in
terms of the model indicator conditional log model evidence
\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})

and the variational parameters of
q(\pi)
.

We first note that for
i=1,...,n

\displaystyle I_{\mu_{i}}

\displaystyle=\int q(\pi)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\ln p(\pi,\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n})d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}d\pi

\displaystyle=\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\ln p(\pi,\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n})d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}d\pi

\displaystyle=\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\ln\left(p(\pi)\prod_{k=1}^{n}p(\mu_{k}|\pi)p(y_{k}|\mu_{k})\right)d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}d\pi

\displaystyle=\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\left(\ln p(\pi)+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\ln p(\mu_{k}|\pi)+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\ln p(y_{k}|\mu_{k})\right)d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}d\pi

\displaystyle=\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\ln p(\pi)+\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\sum_{k=1}^{n}\ln p(\mu_{k}|\pi)+\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\sum_{k=1}^{n}\ln p(y_{k}|\mu_{k})\,d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}\,d\pi

\displaystyle=\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\ln p(\pi)d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\sum_{k=1}^{n}\ln p(\mu_{k}|\pi)d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\sum_{k=1}^{n}\ln p(y_{k}|\mu_{k})d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}d\pi

\displaystyle=\int q(\pi)\ln p(\pi)\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\ln p(\mu_{k}|\pi)+\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\ln p(y_{k}|\mu_{k})+\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}d\pi

\displaystyle=\int q(\pi)\ln p(\pi)d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\int q(\pi)\left(\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\ln p(\mu_{k}|\pi)d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}+\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}\right)d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\int q(\pi)\left(\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\ln p(y_{k}|\mu_{k})d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}+\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}\right)d\pi

\displaystyle=\int q(\pi)\ln p(\pi)d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\ln p(\mu_{k}|\pi)d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}d\pi+\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\ln p(y_{k}|\mu_{k})d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}d\pi+\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}d\pi

\displaystyle=\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}d\pi+\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}d\pi+c

\displaystyle=\int q(\pi)\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}d\pi+\int q(\pi)\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}d\pi+c

\displaystyle=\int q(\pi)\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)\,d\pi+\int q(\pi)\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\,d\pi+c

\displaystyle=\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})+\int q(\pi)\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)\,d\pi+c,

where we defined the constant term

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle:=\int q(\pi)\ln p(\pi)d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\ln p(\mu_{k}|\pi)d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}q(\mu_{k})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1\\
k\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\ln p(y_{k}|\mu_{k})d\mu_{1:n\backslash i}d\pi,

which does not vary with
\mu_{i}
.

For the remaining integral term, we then obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int q(\pi)\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)\,d\pi&=\int q(\pi)\ln\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{j}^{\mu_{ij}}\right)\,d\pi\\
&=\int q(\pi)\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{ij}\ln\pi_{j}\,d\pi\\
&=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{ij}\int q(\pi)\ln\pi_{j}\,d\pi\\
&=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{ij}\left(\psi\left(\alpha_{j}^{q}\right)-\psi\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}^{q}\right)\right)\\
&:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{ij}\left(\psi\left(\alpha_{j}^{q}\right)-\psi\left(\alpha_{s}^{q}\right)\right),\\
\end{split}

(S.252)

where
\psi
denotes the Digamma function. Here, the last equality follows from the
well-known properties of the Dirichlet distribution. Taken together, we thus
obtain for
\mu_{i}\in E_{m}

I(\mu_{i})=\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{ij}\left(\psi\left(\alpha_{j}^{q}\right)-\psi\left(\alpha_{s}^{q}\right)\right)=\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})+\psi\left(\alpha_{j}^{q}\right)+\psi\left(\alpha_{s}^{q}\right),

(S.253)

where the last equality follows from the fact that for
\mu_{i}=e_{j},j=1,...,m
it holds that

\mu_{ij}=1
for
i=j

\mu_{ij}=0
for
i\neq j
. For the variational update
of
q(\mu_{i})
, we thus obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}q(\mu_{i})&=\frac{1}{\gamma_{\mu_{i}}}\exp\left(I_{\mu_{i}}\right)\\
\Leftrightarrow q(\mu_{i})&=\frac{1}{\gamma_{\mu_{i}}}\exp\left(\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})+\psi\left(\alpha_{j}^{q}\right)-\psi\left(\alpha_{s}^{q}\right)\right)\\
\Leftrightarrow q(\mu_{i})&=\frac{\exp\left(\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})+\psi\left(\alpha_{j}^{q}\right)-\psi\left(\alpha_{s}^{q}\right)\right)}{\sum_{\mu_{i}\in E_{m}}\exp\left(\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})+\psi\left(\alpha_{j}^{q}\right)-\psi\left(\alpha_{s}^{q}\right)\right)}.\end{split}

(S.254)

Finally, with
\mu_{i}\in E_{m}
, we may re-express the above in Multinoulli distribution
form by defining

\pi_{ij}^{q}:=\frac{\exp\left(\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i}=e_{j})+\psi\left(\alpha_{j}^{q}\right)-\psi\left(\alpha_{s}^{q}\right)\right)}{\sum_{\mu_{i}\in E_{m}}\exp\left(\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})+\psi\left(\alpha_{j}^{q}\right)-\psi\left(\alpha_{s}^{q}\right)\right)},

(S.255)

such that

q(\mu_{i})=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\left(\pi_{ij}^{q}\right)^{\mu_{i_{j}}}.

(S.256)

In summary, we have, by re-introducing the algorithm iteration superscripts instead
of the variational superscript
q
, as well as the hypothesis subscript

q^{(k+1)}_{1}(\mu_{i})=\mbox{Mult}\left(\mu_{i};\pi_{i}^{(k+1)}\right),\mbox{ where }\pi_{i}^{(k+1)}:=\left(\pi_{ij}^{(k+1)}\right)_{j=1,...,m}

(S.257)

with

\pi_{ij}^{(k+1)}:=\frac{\exp\left(\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i}=e_{j})+\psi\left(\alpha_{j}^{(k)}\right)-\psi\left(\alpha_{s}^{(k)}\right)\right)}{\sum_{\mu_{i}\in E_{m}}\exp\left(\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})+\psi\left(\alpha_{j}^{(k)}\right)-\psi\left(\alpha_{s}^{(k)}\right)\right)}.

(S.258)

\hfill\Box

Proof of eq. (
S.194
)

From
Section S10.2
and the definitions of
eq. (
S.188
) and eq. (
S.189
) in the current inference scenario, we see that the
CAVI algorithm update for the variational distribution on
\pi
takes the form

q(\pi):=\frac{1}{\gamma_{\pi}}\exp\left(I_{\pi}\right)\mbox{ with }I_{\pi}:=\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\ln p(\pi,\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n})\,d\mu_{1:n},

(S.259)

where here and in the following, we omit iteration superscripts and hypothesis
subscripts for ease of notation. To show the validity of eq. (
S.194
), we proceed in two steps.
In Step (1), we first rewrite
I_{\pi}
in terms of the variational probabilities

\pi_{ij}^{q}:=q(\mu_{i})\mbox{ for }\mu_{i}=e_{j}\mbox{ with }e_{j}\in E_{m},

(S.260)

i.e., the variational probability of the
i
th participant model indicator corresponding
to the
j
th
m
-dimensional canonical unit vector. In Step (2), we then
substitute the resulting form of
I_{\pi}
in eq. (
S.259
) and derive the Dirichlet
form for the variational distribution on
\pi
.

Step (1)

We first note that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&I_{\pi}\\
&:=\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\ln p(\pi,\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n})\,d\mu_{1:n}\\
&=\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\ln\left(p(\pi)\prod_{i=1}^{n}p(\mu_{i}|\pi)p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\right)\,d\mu_{1:n}\\
&=\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\left(\ln p(\pi)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\right)\,d\mu_{1:n}\\
&=\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\ln p(\pi)\,d\mu_{1:n}+\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)\,d\mu_{1:n}+\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\,d\mu_{1:n}\\
&=\ln p(\pi)\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\,d\mu_{1:n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int q(\mu_{i})\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)\,d\mu_{i}+c_{1}\\
&=\ln p(\pi)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int q(\mu_{i})\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)\,d\mu_{i}+c_{1},\end{split}

(S.261)

where we have defined the constant term

c_{1}:=\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\,d\mu_{1:n},

(S.262)

which does not depend on
\pi
.

Substituting the functional forms of
p(\pi)
and
p(\mu_{i}|\pi)
yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}I_{\pi}&=\ln\left(\frac{\Gamma\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}\right)}{\prod_{j=1}^{m}\Gamma(\alpha_{j})}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{j}^{\alpha_{j}-1}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int q(\mu_{i})\ln\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{j}^{\mu_{ij}}\right)\,d\mu_{i}+c_{1}\\
&=\ln\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{j}^{\alpha_{j}-1}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int q(\mu_{i})\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{ij}\ln\pi_{j}\right)\,d\mu_{i}+c_{1}+c_{2}\\
&=\sum_{j=1}^{m}(\alpha_{j}-1)\ln\pi_{j}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int q(\mu_{i})\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{ij}\ln\pi_{j}\right)\,d\mu_{i}+c_{3},\end{split}

(S.263)

where we defined the additional constant terms

c_{2}:=\ln\left(\frac{\Gamma\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}\right)}{\prod_{j=1}^{m}\Gamma(\alpha_{j})}\right)\mbox{ and }c_{3}:=c_{1}+c_{2},

(S.264)

which do not vary with
\pi
. We next consider the remaining integral term
in further detail. Because
\mu_{i}
is by definition an element of the set of

m
-dimensional canonical unit vectors
E_{m}
, we have

\int q(\mu_{i})\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{ij}\ln\pi_{j}\right)\,d\mu_{i}=\sum_{\mu_{i}\in E_{m}}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{ij}\ln\pi_{j}=\sum_{\mu_{i}\in E_{m}}q(\mu_{i})\ln\pi_{j},

(S.265)

where the last equality follows from the fact that
\mu_{ij}=0
for
j\neq i
and
\mu_{ij}=1
for
j=i
. Next, let

\pi_{ij}^{q}:=q(\mu_{i})\mbox{ for }\mu_{i}=e_{j}\mbox{ with }e_{j}\in E_{m},

(S.266)

such that
\pi_{ij}^{q}
denotes the probability of
\mu_{i}
being the
j
th
m
-dimensional
canonical unit vector. Then,

\int q(\mu_{i})\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{ij}\ln\pi_{j}\right)\,d\mu_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{q}\ln\pi_{j}.

(S.267)

We thus obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}I_{\pi}&=\sum_{j=1}^{m}(\alpha_{j}-1)\ln\pi_{j}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{q}\ln\pi_{j}+c_{3}\\
&=\sum_{j=1}^{m}(\alpha_{j}-1)\ln\pi_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{ij}^{q}\ln\pi_{j}+c_{3}\\
&=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left((\alpha_{j}-1)\ln\pi_{j}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{ij}^{q}\ln\pi_{j}\right)+c_{3}\\
&=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\alpha_{j}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{ij}^{q}-1\right)\ln\pi_{j}+c_{3}\\
&=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\alpha_{j}+\beta_{j}-1\right)\ln\pi_{j}+c_{3},\end{split}

(S.268)

where we defined

\beta_{j}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{ij}^{q}.

(S.269)

Step (2)

Substitution of
I_{\pi}
in eq. (
S.259
) yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}q(\pi)&=\frac{1}{\gamma_{\pi}}\exp(I_{\pi})\\
\Leftrightarrow\ln q(\pi)&=\ln(\exp(I_{\pi}))-\ln\gamma_{\pi}\\
\Leftrightarrow\ln q(\pi)&=I_{\pi}-\ln\gamma_{\pi}\\
\Leftrightarrow\ln q(\pi)&=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\alpha_{j}+\beta_{j}-1\right)\ln\pi_{j}+c_{3}-\ln\gamma_{\pi}\\
\Leftrightarrow\ln q(\pi)&=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\alpha_{j}+\beta_{j}-1\right)\ln\pi_{j}+c_{4},\\
\end{split}

(S.270)

where we defined

c_{4}:=c_{3}-\ln\gamma_{\pi}.

(S.271)

We thus see that the logarithm of the variational distribution
q(\pi)
is of the form
of the logarithm of the functional kernel of a Dirichlet distribution with parameters

\alpha^{q}_{j}:=\alpha_{j}+\beta_{j}\mbox{ for }j=1,...,m.

(S.272)

As the normalization constant for this kernel is well-known as the normalization constant
of the Dirichlet distribution, we thus have

q(\pi)=\frac{\Gamma\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}^{q}\right)}{\prod_{j=1}^{m}\Gamma(\alpha_{j}^{q})}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{j}^{\alpha_{j}^{q}-1}=\mbox{Dir}(\pi;\alpha^{q}).

(S.273)

In summary, we have, now re-introducing the algorithm iteration superscripts in lieu
of the variational superscript
q
as well as the hypothesis subscript,

q^{(k+1)}_{1}(\pi):=\mbox{Dir}\left(\pi,\alpha^{(k+1)}\right),

(S.274)

where

\alpha^{(k+1)}_{j}:=\alpha_{j}+\beta_{j}^{(k)}\mbox{ with }\beta_{j}^{(k)}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi_{ij}^{(k)}.

(S.275)

\hfill\Box

Proof of eq. (
S.201
)

The ELBO in the current scenario is given by

\mbox{ELBO}(q(\pi,\mu_{1:n})):=\int q(\pi,\mu_{1:n})\ln\left(\frac{p(\pi,\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n})}{q(\pi,\mu_{1:n})}\right)\,d\mu_{1:n}d\pi.

(S.276)

In the following, we first rewrite the right-hand side of eq. (
S.276
) as a
sum of five integral terms. We then consider these integral terms and finally
assemble the form provided in Equation eq. (
S.201
). Thus, we first note that

\displaystyle\mbox{ELBO}(q(\pi,\mu_{1:n}))

\displaystyle:=\int q(\pi,\mu_{1:n})\ln\left(\frac{p(\pi,\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n})}{q(\pi,\mu_{1:n})}\right)\,d\mu_{1:n}d\pi

\displaystyle=\int q(\pi,\mu_{1:n})\left(\ln p(\pi,\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n})-\ln q(\pi,\mu_{1:n})\right)\,d\mu_{1:n}d\pi

\displaystyle=\int q(\pi,\mu_{1:n})\ln p(\pi,\mu_{1:n},y_{1:n})\,d\mu_{1:n}d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt-\int q(\pi,\mu_{1:n})\ln q(\pi,\mu_{1:n})\,d\mu_{1:n}d\pi

\displaystyle=\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\ln\left(p(\pi)\prod_{i=1}^{n}p(\mu_{i}|\pi)p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\right)\,d\mu_{1:n}d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt-\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\ln\left(q(\pi)\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\right)\,d\mu_{1:n}d\pi

\displaystyle=\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\left(\ln p(\pi)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\right)\,d\mu_{1:n}d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt-\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\left(\ln q(\pi)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln q(\mu_{i})\right)\,d\mu_{1:n}d\pi

\displaystyle=\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\ln p(\pi)\,d\mu_{1:n}d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)\,d\mu_{1:n}d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\,d\mu_{1:n}d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt-\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\ln q(\pi)\,d\mu_{1:n}d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt-\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln q(\mu_{i})\,d\mu_{1:n}d\pi

\displaystyle=\int q(\pi)\ln p(\pi)\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\,d\mu_{1:n}d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)\,d\mu_{1:n}d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\,d\mu_{1:n}\int q(\pi)\,d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt-\int q(\pi)\ln q(\pi)\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\,d\mu_{1:n}d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt-\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln q(\mu_{i})\,d\mu_{1:n}\int q(\pi)\,d\pi

\displaystyle=\int q(\pi)\ln p(\pi)\cdot 1d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)\,d\mu_{1:n}d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\,d\mu_{1:n}\cdot 1

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt-\int q(\pi)\ln q(\pi)\cdot 1d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt-\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln q(\mu_{i})\,d\mu_{1:n}\cdot 1

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt=\int q(\pi)\ln p(\pi)d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)\,d\mu_{1:n}d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\,d\mu_{1:n}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt-\int q(\pi)\ln q(\pi)d\pi

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt-\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln q(\mu_{i})\,d\mu_{1:n}.

We next consider the remaining integral terms in turn. To this end, we use the
definition of

\pi_{ij}^{q}:=q(\mu_{i})\mbox{ for }\mu_{i}=e_{j}\mbox{ with }e_{j}\in E_{m}.

(S.277)

repeatedly. For the first term, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}T_{1}&:=\int q(\pi)\ln p(\pi)d\pi\\
&=\int q(\pi)\ln\left(\frac{\Gamma\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}\right)}{\prod_{j=1}^{m}\Gamma(\alpha_{j})}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{j}^{\alpha_{j}-1}\right)\,d\pi\\
&=\int q(\pi)\left(\ln\Gamma\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}\right)-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\ln\Gamma(\alpha_{j})+\sum_{j=1}^{m}(\alpha_{j}-1)\ln\pi_{j}\right)\,d\pi\\
&=\int q(\pi)\ln\Gamma\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}\right)\,d\pi-\int q(\pi)\sum_{j=1}^{m}\ln\Gamma(\alpha_{j})\,d\pi+\int q(\pi)\sum_{j=1}^{m}(\alpha_{j}-1)\ln\pi_{j}\,d\pi\\
&=\ln\Gamma\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}\right)-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\ln\Gamma(\alpha_{j})+\sum_{j=1}^{m}(\alpha_{j}-1)\int q(\pi)\ln\pi_{j}\,d\pi\\
&=\ln\Gamma\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}\right)-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\ln\Gamma(\alpha_{j})+\sum_{j=1}^{m}(\alpha_{j}-1)\left(\psi(\alpha_{j}^{q})-\psi(\alpha_{s}^{q})\right).\end{split}

(S.278)

For the second term, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}T_{2}&:=\int q(\pi)\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)\,d\mu_{1:n}d\pi\\
&=\int q(\pi)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int q(\mu_{i})\ln p(\mu_{i}|\pi)\,d\mu_{i}d\pi\\
&=\int q(\pi)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int q(\mu_{i})\ln\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m}\pi_{j}^{\mu_{ij}}\right)\,d\mu_{i}d\pi\\
&=\int q(\pi)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int q(\mu_{i})\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mu_{ij}\ln\pi_{j}\right)\,d\mu_{i}d\pi\\
&=\int q(\pi)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{\mu_{i}\in E_{m}}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mu_{ij}\ln\pi_{j}d\pi\\
&=\int q(\pi)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{q}\ln\pi_{j}\,d\pi\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{q}\int q(\pi)\ln\pi_{j}\,d\pi\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{q}\left(\psi(\alpha_{j}^{q})-\psi(\alpha_{s}^{q})\right).\end{split}

(S.279)

For the third term, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}T_{3}&:=\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\,d\mu_{1:n}\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int q(\mu_{i})\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\,d\mu_{i}\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{\mu_{i}\in E_{m}}q(\mu_{i})\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{q}l_{ij},\end{split}

(S.280)

where we defined the log model evidence terms

l_{ij}:=\ln p(y_{i}|\mu_{i})\mbox{ for }\mu_{i}=e_{j}\mbox{ with }e_{j}\in E_{m}.

(S.281)

For the fourth term, we have

\displaystyle T_{4}

\displaystyle=\int q(\pi)\ln q(\pi)d\pi

\displaystyle=\int q(\pi)\ln\left(\frac{\Gamma\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}^{q}\right)}{\prod_{j=1}^{m}\Gamma(\alpha_{j}^{q})}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{j}^{\alpha_{j}^{q}-1}\right)\,d\pi

\displaystyle=\int q(\pi)\left(\ln\Gamma\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}^{q}\right)-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\ln\Gamma(\alpha_{j}^{q})+\sum_{j=1}^{m}(\alpha_{j}^{q}-1)\ln\pi_{j}\right)\,d\pi

\displaystyle=\int q(\pi)\ln\Gamma\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}^{q}\right)\,d\pi-\int q(\pi)\sum_{j=1}^{m}\ln\Gamma(\alpha_{j}^{q})\,d\pi+\int q(\pi)\sum_{j=1}^{m}(\alpha_{j}^{q}-1)\ln\pi_{j}\,d\pi

\displaystyle=\ln\Gamma\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}\right)-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\ln\Gamma(\alpha_{j})+\sum_{j=1}^{m}(\alpha_{j}-1)\int q(\pi)\ln\pi_{j}\,d\pi

\displaystyle=\ln\Gamma\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}^{q}\right)-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\ln\Gamma(\alpha_{j}^{q})+\sum_{j=1}^{m}(\alpha_{j}^{q}-1)\left(\psi(\alpha_{j}^{q})-\psi(\alpha_{s}^{q})\right).

Finally, for the fifth term, we have

\displaystyle T_{5}

\displaystyle=\int\prod_{i=1}^{n}q(\mu_{i})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln q(\mu_{i})\,d\mu_{1:n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int q(\mu_{i})\ln q(\mu_{i})\,d\mu_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{\mu_{i}\in E_{m}}q(\mu_{i})\ln q(\mu_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{q}\ln\pi_{ij}^{q}.

In summary, we thus obtain

\displaystyle\mbox{ELBO}(q(\pi,\mu_{1:n}))

\displaystyle=\ln\Gamma\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}\right)-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\ln\Gamma(\alpha_{j})+\sum_{j=1}^{m}(\alpha_{j}-1)\left(\psi(\alpha_{j}^{q})-\psi(\alpha_{s}^{q})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{q}\left(\psi(\alpha_{j}^{q})-\psi(\alpha_{s}^{q})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{q}l_{ij}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt-\ln\Gamma\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}^{q}\right)+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\ln\Gamma(\alpha_{j}^{q})-\sum_{j=1}^{m}(\alpha_{j}^{q}-1)\left(\psi(\alpha_{j}^{q})-\psi(\alpha_{s}^{q})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\pi_{ij}^{q}\ln\pi_{ij}^{q}.

eq. (
S.201
) then follows immediately by replacing the variational superscripts
with the iteration of convergence superscript
(c)
.
